      minhash: AKBZmM7oIQhaFScrXo1ETavQHgUETagiPZxTcVBPKGPNtQ8dbGvDLXn5thvZ2yYPsIb8uni9gF4NGTrHt1+lAV3RyzZurSbVw326POk4FhINdO/VvY9BhZGOe4Fa3bbUMUH1solTB3g9nHRKH/Z9v0Epjln6zgECPj4b+N/5xL0rsxVZEjCLjzYCsewqy3+NvwnCvEZuW8bDeSLxEuiQaBCVOzij4wY6IZTYlHzah86JLiofwdW4LQm7L7eWc9rnowfUaLYveedZxifFT2romegyes4LqHe30sf5Z4jRDFgwkgHy16CC4r2QNXOG34EjD+cz82gDa7enPrwFf+sWww==

---
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{theorem:A}
	Let $(M^n, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension $n \ge 4$. There exist $T = T(g_0) > 0$ and a unique smooth solution $g(t)$ of the $J$-flow on $M^n \times [0,T]$ with $g(0) = g_0$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{theorem:B}
	Let $(M^n, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension $n \ge 4$, and let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^n \times [0, \tau]$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose that $|\mathrm{Rm}_{g(t)}| \le K$ and $\tau \le K^{-2}$. Then for every $m \ge 0$ there exists $C_m = C_m(n, m)$ such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:BBS}
		\left\| \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} \right\|_{L^2(g(t))} \le C_m \left\| \mathrm{Rm} \right\|_{L^2(g(0))} t^{-\frac{m}{4}}
	\end{equation}
	for all $0 < t \le \min\{\tau, 1\}$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{theorem:C}
	Let $(M^n, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension $n \ge 4$. Fix $p\in M$ and $r > 0$, and let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $B_{2r}(p,g_0) \times [0,\tau]$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Assume $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le K$, $\tau \le K^{-2}$. Suppose that there are time-independent smooth cutoffs $0 \le \phi,\Phi \le 1$ supported in $B_{2r}(p,g_0)$, with $\phi = 1$ on $B_r(p,g_0)$ and $\Phi = 1$ on a neighborhood of $\overline{\{ \phi > 0\}}$, satisfying
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:cutoff-estimate}
		\sup_{0 \le t \le \tau} \left(|\nabla \phi| + |\nabla^2 \phi| + |\nabla \Phi| + |\nabla^2 \Phi| \right)_{g(t)} \le L.
	\end{equation}
	Then for every $m \ge 0$ there is $C = C(n,m,K,L)$ such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-BBS}
		\int_{B_r(p,g_0)} |\nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}|_{g(t)}^2 dv_{g(t)} \le C t^{-\frac{m}{2}} \sup_{0 \le s \le t} \int_{B_{2r} (p,g_0)} |\mathrm{Rm}|_{g(s)}^2 dv_{g(s)}
	\end{equation}
	for $0 < t \le \min\{\tau, 1\}$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{theorem:D}
	Let $(M^4, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian four-manifold, and let $g(t)$ be the maximal smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^4 \times [0, T_{\max})$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose there are $C_S < \infty$ and $r_0 > 0$, independent of time, such that for every smooth function $u$ and every $t < T_{\max}$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform-Sobolev-D}
		\|u\|_{L^4(g(t))}^2 \le C_S  \left( \|\nabla u\|_{L^2(g(t))}^2 + r_0^{-2} \|u\|_{L^2(g(t))}^2 \right).
	\end{equation}
	There is $\varepsilon(C_S) > 0$, independent of $g_0$ and $r_0$, such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:global-L2-small}
		\sup_{0 \le t < T_{\max}} \int_M |\mathrm{Rm}_{g(t)}|^2 dv_{g(t)} < \varepsilon(C_S)
	\end{equation}
	implies $T_{\max} = \infty$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{theorem:E}
	Let $(M^4, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian four-manifold, and let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^4 \times [0, \infty)$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose that $|\mathrm{Rm}_{g(t)}| \le K$ for all $t \ge 0$ and that the Sobolev inequality in \Cref{eq:uniform-Sobolev-D} holds for all $t \ge 0$ with time-independent constants $C_S < \infty$ and $r_0 > 0$. If
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-time-integrability}
		\int_0^\infty \|J_{g(t)}\|_{L^2(g(t))} dt < \infty,
	\end{equation}
	Then $g(t)$ converges as $t \to \infty$ in every $C^k(\bar{g})$ norm, for any fixed smooth background metric $\bar{g}$, to a $J$-flat Riemannian metric.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{theorem:F}
	Let $(M^4, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian four-manifold, and let $g(t)$ be the maximal smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^4 \times [0, T_{\max})$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose that the hypotheses of \Cref{theorem:D} hold, so that $T_{\max} = \infty$. Assume that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Q-time-integrability-F}
		\int_0^{\infty} \|Q_{g(t)}\|_{L^2(g(t))} dt < \infty,
	\end{equation}
	and that there exist $C_{\mathrm L} < \infty$ and $0 < \theta \le \frac{1}{2}$ such that, for all sufficiently large $t$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:LS-hypothesis-F}
		\left[ -\mathcal J(g(t)) \right]^{1-\theta} \le C_{\mathrm L} \|B_{g(t)}\|_{L^2(g(t))}.
	\end{equation}
	Then $g(t)$ converges as $t \to \infty$ in every $C^k(\bar{g})$ norm, for any fixed smooth background metric $\bar{g}$, to a $J$-flat Riemannian metric.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:J-tensor}
	Let $(M^n,g)$ be a Riemannian manifold with $n \ge 4$. The $J$-tensor is the symmetric $2$-tensor
	\begin{equation*}
		J_g := - \frac{1}{2} \Gamma_g^*1.
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:J-explicit}
	Let $(M^n, g)$ be a Riemannian manifold with $n \ge 4$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		J_g = -\frac{1}{2} \Gamma_g^*1 = \frac{1}{n} Q_g g - \frac{1}{n-2} B_g - \frac{n-4}{4 (n-1) (n-2)} T_g.
	\end{equation*}
	In particular, when $n = 4$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-dim4}
		J_g = \frac{1}{4} Q_g g - \frac{1}{2} B_g,
	\end{equation}
	and the $J$-flow becomes
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-flow-dim4-again}
		\frac{\partial g}{\partial t} = -2 J_g = B_g - \frac{1}{2} Q_g g.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:trace-J}
	For any Riemannian manifold $(M^n, g)$ with $n \ge 4$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{tr}_g J_g = Q_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Consequently, along the $J$-flow, the volume element evolves by
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:volume-evolution}
		\frac{\partial}{\partial t} dv_g = -Q_g dv_g.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Both $B_g$ and $T_g$ are trace-free, so tracing \Cref{prop:J-explicit} gives $\operatorname{tr}_g J_g = Q_g$. The volume formula follows from $\partial_t dv_g = \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}_g(\partial_t g) dv_g$ and $\partial_t g = -2 J_g$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:J-Schur}
	The tensor $J_g$ satisfies $\operatorname{div}_g J_g = \frac{1}{4} dQ_g$. Consequently a $J$-Einstein metric has constant $Q$ on each connected component when $n \ne 4$. In dimension four,
	\begin{equation*}
		J_g \text{-Einstein} \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad B_g = 0,
	\end{equation*}
	and $J$-Einstein with constant coefficient is equivalent to $B_g = 0$ and $Q_g$ constant.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The functional $\int_M Q_g dv_g$ is invariant under pullback by diffeomorphisms, and its first variation is the $L^2$-pairing with $-2 J + \frac{1}{2} Qg$. Pairing this tensor with a Lie derivative and integrating by parts shows that
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{div}(-2 J + \frac{1}{2} Q g) = 0.
	\end{equation*}
	For $J$-Einstein, $J = \frac{1}{n} Q g$, this becomes $(\frac{1}{n} - \frac{1}{4}) dQ = 0$. At $n = 4$ the equation is an identity, and both assertions follow instead from \Cref{eq:J-dim4}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:J-full}
	The $J$-tensor admits the expression
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-full}
		\begin{split}
			J_g 
			& = -\frac{(3n-4)(n-4)}{8(n-1)^2(n-2)^2} g \Delta_g R_g - \frac{n^2-10n+12}{8(n-1)^2(n-2)} \nabla_g^2 R_g - \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \Delta_g \mathrm{Ric}_g \\
			& \quad - \frac{2}{(n-2)^2} \mathring{\mathrm{Rm}}_g \cdot \mathrm{Ric}_g + \frac{n^2(n-4)+16(n-1)}{8(n-1)^2(n-2)^2} R_g \mathrm{Ric}_g.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:J-dim4-full}
	When $n = 4$, formula~\Cref{eq:J-full} specializes to
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-dim4-full}
		J_g = \frac{1}{12} \nabla_g^2 R_g - \frac{1}{4} \Delta_g \mathrm{Ric}_g - \frac{1}{2} \mathring{\mathrm{Rm}}_g \cdot \mathrm{Ric}_g + \frac{1}{6} R_g \mathrm{Ric}_g.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:product-curvature}
	Let $(M_1^2, g_1)$ and $(M_2^2, g_2)$ be surfaces of constant Gauss curvature $\kappa_1$ and $\kappa_2$ respectively, and let $g = g_1 \oplus g_2$ on $M = M_1 \times M_2$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		Q_g = -\frac{1}{3} \left(\kappa_1^2 - 4 \kappa_1 \kappa_2 + \kappa_2^2 \right),
	\end{equation*}
	the Bach tensor is diagonal with
	\begin{equation*}
		B_g = \left( \frac{\kappa_1^2 - \kappa_2^2}{6} \right)  g_1 \oplus \left( -\frac{\kappa_1^2 - \kappa_2^2}{6} \right) g_2,
	\end{equation*}
	and consequently
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:product-J}
		J_g = \left( -\frac{\kappa_1(\kappa_1 - 2\kappa_2)}{6} \right) g_1  \oplus \left( -\frac{\kappa_2(\kappa_2 - 2 \kappa_1)}{6} \right) g_2 .
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Fix a local orthonormal frame $\{e_1, e_2, e_3, e_4\}$ with $e_1, e_2$ tangent to $M_1$ and $e_3, e_4$ tangent to $M_2$. Up to the symmetries of $\mathrm{Rm}$, the only non-vanishing curvature components are $R_{1221} = \kappa_1$ and $R_{3443} = \kappa_2$, whence
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathrm{Ric} = \operatorname{diag}(\kappa_1, \kappa_1, \kappa_2, \kappa_2), \qquad R = 2(\kappa_1 + \kappa_2), \qquad | \mathrm{Ric} |^2 = 2 \kappa_1^2 + 2 \kappa_2^2 .
	\end{equation*}
	Since $R$ is constant, formula \Cref{eq:Q-dim4-intro} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		Q_g = -\frac{1}{2} \left(2 \kappa_1^2 + 2 \kappa_2^2 \right) + \frac{1}{6} \cdot 4( \kappa_1 + \kappa_2)^2 = -\frac{1}{3} \left(\kappa_1^2 - 4 \kappa_1 \kappa_2 + \kappa_2^2 \right).
	\end{equation*}
	Next we compute the Weyl tensor from the decomposition in \Cref{eq:Rm-decomp}. The traceless Ricci tensor is 
	\begin{equation*}
		E = \operatorname{diag}(\frac{\kappa_1 - \kappa_2}{2}, \frac{\kappa_1 - \kappa_2}{2}, - \frac{\kappa_1 - \kappa_2}{2}, -\frac{\kappa_1 - \kappa_2}{2}).
	\end{equation*}
	A direct evaluation of the Kulkarni--Nomizu products in the frame above yields
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:product-Weyl}
		W_{1221} = W_{3443} = \frac{\kappa_1 + \kappa_2}{3}, \qquad W_{1331} = W_{1441} = W_{2332} = W_{2442} = -\frac{\kappa_1 + \kappa_2}{6},
	\end{equation}
	with all components not obtained from these by the symmetries of $W$ equal to zero. Because $g$ is a product of constant-curvature metrics it is locally symmetric, so $\nabla W = 0$ and the classical formula \Cref{eq:Bach-classical} reduces to the zeroth-order term
	\begin{equation*}
		B_{ij} = \frac{1}{2} R^{kl} W_{iklj} .
	\end{equation*}
	Using \Cref{eq:product-Weyl},
	\begin{equation*}
		B_{11} = \frac{1}{2} \left( \kappa_1 W_{1221} + \kappa_2 W_{1331} + \kappa_2 W_{1441} \right) = \frac{1}{2}( \kappa_1 + \kappa_2) \left(\frac{\kappa_1}{3} - \frac{\kappa_2}{3} \right) = \frac{\kappa_1^2 - \kappa_2^2}{6},
	\end{equation*}
	and the analogous computation gives $B_{33} = -B_{11}$. As consistency checks, $B$ vanishes when $\kappa_1 = \kappa_2$ (the Einstein case) and when $\kappa_1 = -\kappa_2$, where \Cref{eq:product-Weyl} shows $W \equiv 0$, so the metric is locally conformally flat and scalar-flat. Finally, \Cref{eq:J-dim4} gives, on the first factor,
	\begin{equation*}
		J_{11} = \frac{Q_g}{4} - \frac{B_{11}}{2} = -\frac{\kappa_1^2 - 4\kappa_1 \kappa_2 + \kappa_2^2}{12} - \frac{\kappa_1^2 - \kappa_2^2}{12} = -\frac{\kappa_1( \kappa_1 - 2 \kappa_2)}{6},
	\end{equation*}
	and similarly on the second factor, proving \Cref{eq:product-J}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:ungauged-linearization}
	The linearization of the ungauged $J$-flow operator $-2 J_g$ at $g$ has principal part
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:ungauged-linearization}
		\begin{split}
			\left(D( -2J_g) \cdot h \right)_{ij}
			=& -\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \left( \Delta^2 h_{ij} + \nabla_i \Delta(\delta h)_j + \nabla_j \Delta(\delta h)_i \right) - \alpha_n \nabla_i \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_gh + \beta_n \nabla_i \nabla_j \delta^2h \\
			&- \gamma_n g_{ij} \left( \Delta^2 \operatorname{tr}_g h - \Delta \delta^2 h \right) + \text{l.o.t.}
		\end{split}
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The highest-order part of $-2 J_g$ is given by \Cref{eq:J-principal}. We linearize each of the three fourth-order summands separately, retaining only terms of differential order four in $h$. Throughout, commutators of covariant derivatives with the linearization produce curvature contractions against at most three derivatives of $h$, and are therefore absorbed into the remainder.

	We begin with the Hessian of the scalar curvature. The linearization of scalar curvature is~\Cref{eq:R-linearization}. Discarding the zeroth-order term $\mathrm{Ric}\cdot h$, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		D R_g \cdot h = -\Delta \operatorname{tr}_g h + \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.},
	\end{equation*}
	which yields
	\begin{equation*}
		D(\nabla_i \nabla_j R) \cdot h = \nabla_i \nabla_j (DR_g \cdot h) = -\nabla_i \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_gh + \nabla_i \nabla_j \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation*}
	Next we treat the rough Laplacian of the Ricci tensor. From~\Cref{eq:Ric-linearization},
	\begin{equation*}
		(D \mathrm{Ric}_g \cdot h)_{ij} = -\frac{1}{2} \left( \Delta h_{ij} + \nabla_i(\delta h)_j + \nabla_j(\delta h)_i + \nabla_i \nabla_j \operatorname{tr}_g h \right) + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation*}
	Hence
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(D(\Delta \mathrm{Ric}) \cdot h \right)_{ij}
		= \Delta \left(D \mathrm{Ric}_g \cdot h \right)_{ij} + \text{l.o.t.} 
		= -\frac{1}{2} \left( \Delta^2h_{ij} + \nabla_i \Delta(\delta h)_j + \nabla_j \Delta(\delta h)_i + \nabla_i \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_g h \right) + \text{l.o.t.},
	\end{equation*}
	where we have used $\Delta \nabla_i (\delta h)_j = \nabla_i \Delta (\delta h)_j + \text{l.o.t.}$. It remains to linearize the pure-trace term $g\Delta R$, the product rule gives
	\begin{equation*}
		D(g \Delta R) \cdot h = (D g \cdot h) \Delta R + g (D (\Delta R) \cdot h).
	\end{equation*}
	The first summand is zeroth order in the derivatives of $h$. For the second, the variation of the scalar Laplacian applied to the fixed function $R$ produces at most first-order terms in $h$, while
	\begin{equation*}
		D(\Delta R) \cdot h = \Delta (D R_g \cdot h) + \text{l.o.t.} = -\Delta^2 \operatorname{tr}_gh + \Delta \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation*}
	Consequently
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(D( g \Delta R ) \cdot h \right)_{ij} = g_{ij} \left( -\Delta^2 \operatorname{tr}_g h + \Delta \delta^2 h \right) + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation*}
	Collecting these three contributions against the coefficients in~\Cref{eq:J-principal}, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\left(D( -2J_g) \cdot h \right)_{ij}
			=& \frac{2}{(n-2)^2} \left(D (\Delta \mathrm{Ric}) \cdot h \right)_{ij} + \beta_n D(\nabla_i \nabla_j R) \cdot h + \gamma_n D(g_{ij} \Delta R) \cdot h + \text{l.o.t.} \\
			=& -\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \left( \Delta^2 h_{ij} + \nabla_i \Delta(\delta h)_j + \nabla_j \Delta(\delta h)_i \right) - \left(\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} + \beta_n \right) \nabla_i \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_gh \\
			&+ \beta_n \nabla_i \nabla_j \delta^2h - \gamma_n g_{ij} \left( \Delta^2 \operatorname{tr}_g h - \Delta \delta^2 h \right) + \text{l.o.t.}
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	The algebraic identity $\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} + \beta_n = \alpha_n$ yields~\Cref{eq:ungauged-linearization}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:biharmonic-gauge}
	The biharmonic DeTurck gauge vector field $X$ is defined by the covector
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:biharmonic-X}
		X_j := \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \Delta_g V_j - \frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_j R_g .
	\end{equation}
	The biharmonic-gauged $J$-flow is
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-DeTurck}
		\frac{\partial}{\partial t} g = -2 J_g + \mathcal{L}_X g .
	\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:gauge-linearization}
	The linearization of the gauge term $\mathcal{L}_X g$ at $g$ satisfies
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauge-linearization}
		\left(D (\mathcal{L}_X g) \cdot h \right)_{ij} = \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \left( \nabla_i \Delta (\delta h)_j + \nabla_j \Delta (\delta h)_i \right) + \alpha_n \nabla_i \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_g h - \beta_n \nabla_i \nabla_j \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We first compute the linearization of the gauge vector field $X$. Applying the rough Laplacian to the linearization of $V$ and commuting derivatives gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\Delta((DV \cdot h)_j) = \Delta(\delta h)_j + \frac{1}{2} \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_gh + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation*}
	The scalar-curvature correction contributes
	\begin{equation*}
		-\frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_j(D R_g \cdot h) = \frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_g h - \frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_j \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation*}
	Adding these and using $\alpha_n = \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} + \beta_n$, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			(DX \cdot h)_j
			 & = \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \left( \Delta(\delta h)_j + \frac{1}{2} \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_g h \right) + \frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_g h - \frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_j \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.} \\
			 & = \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \Delta(\delta h)_j + \frac{\alpha_n}{2} \nabla_j \Delta \operatorname{tr}_g h - \frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_j \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.}
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Passing to the Lie derivative, recall that $(\mathcal{L}_X g)_{ij} = \nabla_i X_j + \nabla_j X_i$. Since the variation of the Levi-Civita connection contributes only first-order terms in $h$, one has
	\begin{equation*}
		\left( D(\mathcal{L}_X g) \cdot h \right)_{ij} = \nabla_i (D X \cdot h)_j + \nabla_j(D X \cdot h)_i + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation*}
	Substituting the expression for $D X \cdot h$ obtained above and the result follows.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:linearized-symbol}
	The linearization of the biharmonic-gauged $J$-flow operator at $g$ has principal part
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauged-principal}
		\left(D( -2J_g + \mathcal{L}_X g) \cdot h \right)_{ij} = -\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \Delta^2 h_{ij} - \gamma_n g_{ij} \Delta^2 \operatorname{tr}_g h + \gamma_n g_{ij} \Delta \delta^2 h + \text{l.o.t.}
	\end{equation}
	In particular, the system \Cref{eq:J-DeTurck} is strictly parabolic on the bundle of symmetric $2$-tensors.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Adding~\Cref{eq:ungauged-linearization,eq:gauge-linearization}, the mixed fourth-order terms cancel identically. What remains is precisely~\Cref{eq:gauged-principal}. It remains to check that the principal symbol $\sigma_{\xi}$ is negative definite. 
	
	Let $\xi \in T_p^*M \setminus \{0\}$ and let $H \in \mathrm{Sym}^2 (T_p^*M)$. With the convention $\Delta = g^{ij} \nabla_i \nabla_j$, one has
	\begin{equation*}
		\sigma_{\xi} (\Delta) = -|\xi|^2, \qquad \sigma_{\xi} (\Delta^2) = |\xi|^4.
	\end{equation*}
	The divergence satisfies
	\begin{equation*}
		\left( \sigma_{\xi} (\delta) H \right)_j = -i \xi^p H_{pj}, \quad \sigma_{\xi}(\delta^2) H = -\xi^p \xi^q H_{pq}, \quad \sigma_{\xi}(\Delta \delta^2) H = | \xi|^2 \xi^p \xi^q H_{pq}.
	\end{equation*}
	The principal symbol of the right-hand side of~\Cref{eq:gauged-principal} is consequently the endomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(\sigma_{\xi}(H) \right)_{ij} = -\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} | \xi|^4 H_{ij} - \gamma_n| \xi|^4 (\operatorname{tr}_g H) g_{ij} + \gamma_n| \xi|^2 \xi^p \xi^q H_{pq} g_{ij}.
	\end{equation*}
	We claim that $\langle \sigma_{\xi} (H), H \rangle_g < 0$ whenever $H \neq 0$. Expanding,
	\begin{equation*}
		\langle \sigma_{\xi}(H),H \rangle_g = -\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} | \xi|^4 |H|_g^2 - \gamma_n | \xi|^4 (\operatorname{tr}_g H)^2 + \gamma_n | \xi|^2 \xi^p \xi^q H_{pq} (\operatorname{tr}_g H).
	\end{equation*}
	If $\operatorname{tr}_g H = 0$, the last two terms vanish and the first is strictly negative. If $H = \mu g$ is pure trace, then $\operatorname{tr}_g H = n \mu$, $|H|_g^2 = n \mu^2$, and $\xi^p \xi^q H_{pq} = \mu |\xi|^2$, so $\sigma_{\xi} (\mu g) = \lambda_n (\xi) (\mu g)$, where
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:pure-trace-eigenvalue}
		\lambda_n(\xi) = -\left(\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} + (n - 1) \gamma_n \right) | \xi|^4 = -\frac{4(n-1) + (3n-4)(n-4)}{4(n-1)(n-2)^2} | \xi|^4 = -\frac{3}{4(n-1)} | \xi|^4,
	\end{equation}
	which is strictly negative for every $n\ge4$ and $\mu\neq0$. In particular $\gamma_4=0$ yields $\lambda_4 = -\frac{|\xi|^4}{4}$. For a general $H$, the cross term satisfies
	\begin{equation*}
		\left| \gamma_n| \xi|^2 \xi^p \xi^q H_{pq} (\operatorname{tr}_g H) \right| \le \gamma_n| \xi|^4 |H|_g | \operatorname{tr}_g H|,
	\end{equation*}
	and Young's inequality yields
	\begin{equation*}
		-\gamma_n | \xi|^4 (\operatorname{tr}_g H)^2 + \gamma_n | \xi|^4 |H|_g  |\operatorname{tr}_g H| \le \frac{1}{2} \gamma_n| \xi|^4 |H|_g^2.
	\end{equation*}
	Hence
	\begin{equation*}
		\langle \sigma_{\xi}(H), H \rangle_g \le - \left( \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} - \frac{1}{2} \gamma_n \right) | \xi|^4 |H|_g^2.
	\end{equation*}
	For $n = 4$ one has $\gamma_4 = 0$. For $n \ge 5$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} - \frac{1}{2} \gamma_n = \frac{8(n-1)^2-(3n-4)(n-4)}{8(n-1)^2(n-2)^2} = \frac{5n^2-8}{8(n-1)^2(n-2)^2} > 0.
	\end{equation*}
	Thus $\langle \sigma_{\xi}(H),H \rangle_g \le - c_n |\xi|^4 |H|_g^2$ with $c_n > 0$, so $\sigma_{\xi}$ is negative definite. Therefore~\Cref{eq:J-DeTurck} is a strictly parabolic fourth-order system.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Fix a smooth background metric $\tilde g = g_0$. The operator $-2 J_g + \mathcal L_{X(g, \tilde g)}g$ is a smooth quasilinear fourth-order operator with strongly parabolic linearization by \Cref{thm:linearized-symbol}. The parabolic construction in the proof of Bahuaud--Helliwell~\cite[Theorem~A]{BahuaudHelliwell2011} therefore provides a solution $\bar g(t)$ of \Cref{eq:J-DeTurck}. Equivalently, one may use fourth-order parabolic Schauder theory in $C^{4 + \alpha, 1 + \alpha/4}(M \times [0,T])$, with the initial trace prescribed. Positivity persists for short time and parabolic regularity gives smoothness for smooth initial data.

	Let $\varphi_t$ solve
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_t \varphi_t = -X(\bar g(t), \tilde g) \circ \varphi_t, \qquad \varphi_0 = \mathrm{id}.
	\end{equation*}
	For a time-dependent tensor $h_t$ and a diffeomorphism satisfying $\partial_t \psi_t = Y_t \circ \psi_t$, we have
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:pullback-pushforward}
		\begin{split}
		\frac d{dt}( \psi_t^* h_t) &= \psi_t^* (\partial_t h_t + \mathcal L_{Y_t}h_t),\\
		\frac d{dt}((\psi_t)_* h_t) &= (\psi_t)_* (\partial_t h_t) - \mathcal L_{Y_t} ( (\psi_t)_* h_t).
		\end{split}
	\end{equation}
	Naturality of $J$ and the first identity imply that $g(t) = \varphi_t^* \bar g(t)$ solves $\partial_t g = -2 J_g$ with $g(0) = g_0$.

	For uniqueness we apply the intrinsic uniqueness theorem~\cite[Theorem~A]{BahuaudHelliwell2015}, verifying its coefficients. Its fourth-order class has the form
	\begin{equation*}
		T_g = c \left(\Delta \mathrm{Ric} + a( \Delta R) g - b \nabla^2 R \right) + T_{\le 3}, \qquad c>0,\quad a > -\frac{1}{2(n-1)}.
	\end{equation*}
	Here $T_{\le 3}$ is a polynomial natural tensor of lower differential order. By \Cref{eq:J-principal}, our choices are
	\begin{equation*}
		c = \frac2{(n-2)^2}, \qquad a = \frac{\gamma_n}{c} \ge 0, \qquad b = -\frac{\beta_n}{c},
	\end{equation*}
	and the remaining terms are quadratic curvature contractions. All hypotheses are satisfied for $n \ge 4 $. The theorem therefore gives uniqueness of the ungauged solution.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:Rm-evolution}
	Let $g(t)$ be a solution of the $J$-flow on $M \times [0,T)$ with $n \ge 4$. Then the Riemann curvature tensor satisfies the schematic evolution equation
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Rm-evolution}
		\frac{\partial}{\partial t} \mathrm{Rm} = -\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \Delta^2 \mathrm{Rm} - \frac{\gamma_n}{2} g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R + P_{2,2}(\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{3,0}(\mathrm{Rm}).
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Write $h = -2 J_g$. By \Cref{eq:J-principal} the fourth-order part of $h$ is
	\begin{equation*}
		h^{(4)}_{ij} = \frac{2}{(n-2)^2} (\Delta \mathrm{Ric})_{ij} + \beta_n \nabla_i \nabla_j R + \gamma_n g_{ij} \Delta R .
	\end{equation*}
	We substitute this into the first group of terms in the variational formula \Cref{eq:Rm-variation}. A typical term is
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{2} \nabla_i \nabla_k h^{(4)}_{jl} = \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \nabla_i \nabla_k (\Delta \mathrm{Ric})_{jl} + \frac{\beta_n}{2} \nabla_i \nabla_k \nabla_j \nabla_l R + \frac{\gamma_n}{2} g_{jl}  \nabla_i \nabla_k \Delta R .
	\end{equation*}
	The first summand produces
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{(n-2)^2}\left(\nabla_i \nabla_k \Delta R_{jl} + \nabla_j \nabla_l \Delta R_{ik} - \nabla_i \nabla_l \Delta R_{jk} - \nabla_j \nabla_k \Delta R_{il} \right) + P_{2,2}(\mathrm{Rm})  = - \frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \Delta^2R_{ijkl} + P_{2,2}(\mathrm{Rm}).
	\end{equation*}
	In the alternating combination appearing in \Cref{eq:Rm-variation}, the fully symmetric four-derivative parts cancel in pairs, and only commutator terms remain, in which curvature is contracted against at most two derivatives of $\mathrm{Rm}$. The same applies to the Hessian term $\nabla_i \nabla_j R$. By contrast, the pure-trace term $g_{jl} \Delta R$ carries only two derivatives of $R$, so its alternating combination does not vanish. With the Kulkarni--Nomizu convention~\Cref{eq:KN} one has
	\begin{equation*}
		g_{jl} \nabla_i \nabla_k \Delta R + g_{ik} \nabla_j \nabla_l \Delta R - g_{jk}\nabla_i \nabla_l \Delta R - g_{il} \nabla_j \nabla_k \Delta R = -(g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R)_{ijkl}.
	\end{equation*}
	All remaining contributions are absorbed into $P_{2,2}(\mathrm{Rm})$.

	The remaining terms in \Cref{eq:Rm-variation} are of two types. Those coming from $h^{(4)}$ but with fewer than four derivatives, and those coming from the lower-order part of $h$, are of the schematic types $P_{2,2}(\mathrm{Rm})$ and $P_{3,0}(\mathrm{Rm})$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:Rm-derivative-evolution}
	For every integer $m \ge 0$ the $m$-th covariant derivative of the Riemann tensor satisfies
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Rm-derivative-evolution}
		\frac{\partial}{\partial t} \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} = -\frac{1}{(n-2)^2} \Delta^2 \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} - \frac{\gamma_n}{2} \nabla^m (g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R) + P_{2,m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{3,m}(\mathrm{Rm}).
	\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Applying $\nabla^m$ to \Cref{eq:Rm-evolution} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\nabla^m P_{2,2}(\mathrm{Rm}) = P_{2,m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}), \qquad \nabla^m P_{3,0}(\mathrm{Rm}) = P_{3,m}(\mathrm{Rm}).
	\end{equation*}
	We first commute the spatial derivatives. The Ricci commutation identity gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\nabla^m \Delta^2 \mathrm{Rm} = \Delta^2 \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} + P_{2,m+2} (\mathrm{Rm}).
   	\end{equation*}
	It remains to commute the time derivative through $\nabla^m$. For a general metric variation $\partial_t g = h$, one has
	\begin{equation*}
		[\partial_t, \nabla^m] \mathrm{Rm} = \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} \nabla^i( \partial_t \Gamma) * \nabla^{m-1-i} \mathrm{Rm},
	\end{equation*}
	where the sum is empty when $m = 0$. Here $\partial_t \Gamma$
	is a tensor satisfying
	\begin{equation*}
		(\partial_t \Gamma)^k_{ij} = \frac{1}{2} g^{k l} \left( \nabla_i h_{jl} + \nabla_j h_{il} - \nabla_l h_{ij} \right).
	\end{equation*}
	Since $h = -2 J_g = P_{1,2} (\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{2,0} (\mathrm{Rm})$, the quadratic curvature terms in this commutator have total	derivative order $(i+3) + (m-1-i) = m+2$, whereas the cubic curvature terms have total derivative order $(i+1) + (m-1-i) = m$. Consequently,
	\begin{equation*}
		[\partial_t, \nabla^m] \mathrm{Rm} = P_{2,m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{3,m}(\mathrm{Rm}).
	\end{equation*}
	Combining these identities proves \Cref{eq:Rm-derivative-evolution}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:Hamilton-interpolation}
Let $T$ be a smooth tensor of fixed rank on a closed Riemannian $n$-manifold. If $k \ge 1$, $ 2 \le r < \infty$, and $0 \le i \le k$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hamilton-interpolation}
	\| \nabla^i T \|_{p_i} \le C \| T \|_r^{1 - i/k} \| \nabla^k T \|_2^{i/k}, \qquad \frac{1}{p_i} = \frac{1 - i/k}{r} + \frac{i}{2k}.
\end{equation}
The constant depends only on $n,k,r$ and the rank of $T$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:adjacent-interpolation}
	For $j \ge 0$ and any smooth tensor $T$ on a closed manifold,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:adjacent-Young}
	\|\nabla^{j+1} T\|_2^2 \le C(n) \|\nabla^j T\|_2 \|\nabla^{j+2} T\|_2 \le \varepsilon \|\nabla^{j+2} T \|_2^2 + C(n, \varepsilon) \|\nabla^j T\|_2^2.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Integrate by parts once, apply Cauchy--Schwarz and use $|\Delta \nabla^j T | \le \sqrt{n} |\nabla^2 \nabla^j T |$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:polynomial-interpolation}
	Let $m \ge 0$ be an integer and suppose $|T| \le K$. For any scalar contractions of the indicated types and every $\varepsilon > 0$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:polynomial-Young}
		\left| \int_M P_{3, 2m+2} (T) + P_{4, 2m} (T) dv_g \right| \le \varepsilon \| \nabla^{m+2} T \|_2^2 + C_\varepsilon K^{m+2} \| T \|_2^2.
	\end{equation}
	The constant depends only on $n, m, \varepsilon$, the tensor rank and the finitely many contraction coefficients.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
In each complete contraction, if one factor has more than $m+2$ covariant derivatives, integrate its outermost derivative by parts onto the other factors, and repeat until every factor has at most $m + 2$ derivatives. Since the total derivative order is at most $2m+2$, the other factors cannot exceed $m+2$ derivatives during this process. No derivatives are commuted, and $\nabla g = 0$, so no curvature commutator terms arise. As $M$ is closed, there are no boundary terms. The resulting integrals are finite sums of terms of the forms
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M \nabla^{i_1} T \ast \nabla^{i_2} T \ast \nabla^{i_3} T dv_g, 
		\quad \text{and} \quad
		\int_M \nabla^{i_1} T \ast \nabla^{i_2} T \ast \nabla^{i_3} T \ast \nabla^{i_4} T dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Use \Cref{eq:Hamilton-interpolation} with $k = m + 2$ and $r = m + 4$.
	The exponents simplify to
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{p_i} = \frac{1 - i/(m+2)}{m+4} + \frac{i}{2(m+2)} = \frac{i+2}{2(m+4)}, \qquad 0 \le i \le m+2.
	\end{equation*}
	The first term has derivative orders $i_1 + i_2+ i_3 = 2m + 2$ with $i_\nu \le m+2$, so 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{p_{i_1}} + \frac{1}{p_{i_2}} + \frac{1}{p_{i_3}} = \frac{2m+2+6}{2(m+4)} = 1
	\end{equation*}
	therefore,
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\left| \int_M \nabla^{i_1} T \ast \nabla^{i_2} T \ast \nabla^{i_3} T dv_g \right|
			&\le C \left\| \nabla^{i_1} T \right\|_{p_{i_1}} \left\| \nabla^{i_2} T \right\|_{p_{i_2}} \left\| \nabla^{i_3} T \right\|_{p_{i_3}}\\
			&\le C \left\| T \right\|_{m+4}^{3- \frac{i_1 + i_2 + i_3}{m+2}} \| \nabla^{m+2} T \|_2^{\frac{i_1 + i_2 + i_3}{m+2}}\\
			&= C \left\| T \right\|_{m+4}^{\frac{m+4}{m+2}} \| \nabla^{m+2} T \|_2^{\frac{2m+2}{m+2}}\\
			&\le C(n,m,\epsilon) \left\| T \right\|_{m+4}^{m+4}  + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \| \nabla^{m+2} T \|_2^{2} 
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	For the second term, $i_1 + \cdots + i_4 = 2 m$ with $i_\nu \le m+2$, and the corresponding sum is
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{p_{i_1}} + \frac{1}{p_{i_2}} + \frac{1}{p_{i_3}} + \frac{1}{p_{i_4}} = \frac{2m+8}{2(m+4)} = 1,
	\end{equation*}
	it gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\left| \int_M \nabla^{i_1} T \ast \nabla^{i_2} T \ast \nabla^{i_3} T \ast \nabla^{i_4} T dv_g \right|
			&\le C \left\| \nabla^{i_1} T \right\|_{p_{i_1}} \left\| \nabla^{i_2} T \right\|_{p_{i_2}} \left\| \nabla^{i_3} T \right\|_{p_{i_3}} \left\| \nabla^{i_4} T \right\|_{p_{i_4}}\\
			&\le C \left\| T \right\|_{m+4}^{4- \frac{i_1 + i_2 + i_3 + i_4}{m+2}} \| \nabla^{m+2} T \|_2^{\frac{i_1 + i_2 + i_3 + i_4}{m+2}}\\
			&= C \left\| T \right\|_{m+4}^{\frac{2m+8}{m+2}} \| \nabla^{m+2} T \|_2^{\frac{2m}{m+2}} \\
			&\le C(n,m, \epsilon) \left\| T \right\|_{m+4}^{m+4} + \frac{\epsilon}{2}  \| \nabla^{m+2} T \|_2^{2} 
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Finally,
	\begin{equation*}
		\left\| T \right\|_{m+4}^{m+4} = \int_M |T|^{m+4} dv_g \le K^{m+2} \int_M |T|^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	No additive constant or volume factor is introduced.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:basic-energy-inequality}
	If $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le K$ along a solution on a closed manifold, then for every $m \ge 0$ there is $C_m < \infty$, depending only on $n, m$, such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:basic-energy-inequality}
		\mathcal E_m' + a_n \mathcal E_{m+2} \le C_m K^{m+2} \mathcal E_0.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Fix $m$, and all integrals and norms in this proof are taken with respect to $g(t)$. We first differentiate the energy, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal E_m' = 2 \int_M \langle \partial_t \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} \rangle dv_g	+ \int_M P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{4,2m}(\mathrm{Rm})  dv_g,
	\end{equation*}
	For the rough biharmonic term, the absence of a boundary gives exactly
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M \langle \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}, \Delta^2 \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} \rangle dv_g = -\int_M \langle \nabla \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm},\nabla \Delta \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} \rangle dv_g = \int_M | \Delta \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} |^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Substituting \Cref{eq:Rm-derivative-evolution}, we therefore obtain
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:energy-derivative}
		\begin{split}
			\mathcal E_m'
			=& -2 a_n \int_M |\Delta \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g - \gamma_n \int_M \langle \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} ,\nabla^m (g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R) \rangle dv_g\\
			& + \int_M \left( P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{4,2m}(\mathrm{Rm}) \right) dv_g.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation}
	where $a_n=(n-2)^{-2}$. We next compute the scalar-curvature principal term, keeping track of the commutators when $m > 0$. For any symmetric $2$-tensor $H$, the curvature symmetries and \Cref{eq:KN} give
	\begin{equation*}
		\langle \mathrm{Rm},g \odot H \rangle = 4 \langle \mathrm{Ric}, H \rangle.
	\end{equation*}
	Since $\nabla g = 0$, the same contraction identity holds after taking
	$m$ covariant derivatives. Thus
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			I_m := \int_M \left\langle \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla^m (g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R) \right\rangle dv_g = 4 \int_M \left\langle \nabla^m \mathrm{Ric},\nabla^m( \nabla^2 \Delta R) \right\rangle dv_g.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	In the following calculation, the divergences of $\nabla^m \mathrm{Ric}$ are taken in the Ricci slots, leaving the $m$ derivative slots free. The Hessian slots are paired with the Ricci slots, while the remaining derivative slots are paired in their original order. Commuting the two Hessian derivatives past the $m$ covariant derivatives produces terms of type $P_{2,m+2}(\mathrm{Rm})$, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			I_m 
			&= 4 \int_M \left\langle \nabla^m \mathrm{Ric}, \nabla^2( \nabla^m \Delta R) \right\rangle dv_g + \int_M P_{3,2m+2} (\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g\\
			&= 4 \int_M \left\langle \operatorname{div}^2 (\nabla^m \mathrm{Ric}), \nabla^m \Delta R \right\rangle dv_g +\int_M P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Commuting the two divergences past the $m$ covariant derivatives and contracted Bianchi identity gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{div}^2 (\nabla^m \mathrm{Ric}) = \nabla^m( \operatorname{div}^2 \mathrm{Ric}) + P_{2,m}(\mathrm{Rm}) = \frac{1}{2} \nabla^m \Delta R + P_{2,m}(\mathrm{Rm}).
	\end{equation*}
	After pairing with $\nabla^m \Delta R$, the additional term also has type $P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm})$. Consequently,
	\begin{equation*}
		I_m =2 \|\nabla^m \Delta R\|_2^2 + \int_M P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	It follows that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma-perfect-square}
		-\gamma_n \int_M \left\langle \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla^m (g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R)\right\rangle dv_g = -2 \gamma_n \|\nabla^m \Delta R\|_2^2 + \int_M P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g.
	\end{equation}
	When $m = 0$, no commutations are needed. For every $m$, the principal square is nonpositive because $\gamma_n \ge 0$ for $n \ge 4$.

	We now convert the rough-Laplacian into the full $(m+2)$-nd derivative norm as follows
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\int_M |\nabla^2 \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g = \int_M | \Delta \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g + \int_M P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Thus
	\begin{equation*}
		\|\nabla^{m+2} \mathrm{Rm}\|_2^2 \le \|\Delta \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} \|_2^2 + \left| \int_M P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g \right|.
	\end{equation*}
	All the terms above, including those from the changing metric and volume and the rough-Laplacian calculation, have types $P_{3,2m+2}(\mathrm{Rm})$ and $P_{4,2m}(\mathrm{Rm})$. Applying \Cref{lem:polynomial-interpolation} with $T = \mathrm{Rm}$ to these finitely many integrals, with suitably chosen parameters, gives for every $\varepsilon > 0$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal E_m' \le -(2a_n - \varepsilon) \|\nabla^{m+2} \mathrm{Rm}\|_2^2 - 2 \gamma_n \| \nabla^m \Delta R\|_2^2 + C_\varepsilon K^{m+2} \|\mathrm{Rm}\|_2^2.
	\end{equation*}
	Taking $\varepsilon = a_n$ and discarding the nonpositive scalar square proves \Cref{eq:basic-energy-inequality}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:energy-cascade}
	Fix $k \ge 0$ and $0 < \tau \le 1$, and let $H:[0, \tau] \to [0, \infty)$ be a bounded function. Suppose $E_0, E_2, \ldots, E_{2k+2}$ are nonnegative differentiable functions on $[0, \tau]$ satisfying
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:cascade-ODE}
		E_{2j}' + c_j E_{2j+2} \le b_j E_{2j} + d_j H(t), \qquad 0 \le j \le k,
	\end{equation}
	where $c_j > 0$, $b_j, d_j \ge 0$, and 
	\begin{equation*}
		\bar H := \max\left\{ E_0(0), \displaystyle\sup_{0 \le s \le \tau} H(s) \right\} < \infty.
	\end{equation*}
	Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:cascade-bound}
		E_{2k}(t) \le C_k  \bar{H}t^{-k}, \qquad 0< t \le \tau.
	\end{equation}
	The constant depends only on the finitely many coefficients in \Cref{eq:cascade-ODE}.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Set $\alpha_0 = 1$ and choose the positive coefficients successively so that
	$j \alpha_j \le \frac{1}{2} c_{j-1} \alpha_{j-1}$. Define
	\begin{equation*}
		F_k(t) = \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j t^j E_{2j}(t).
	\end{equation*}
	Differentiating each summand and using \Cref{eq:cascade-ODE} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			F_k' \le  - \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j c_j t^j E_{2j+2} + \sum_{j=1}^k j \alpha_j t^{j-1} E_{2j} + \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j b_j t^j E_{2j} + H(t) \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j d_j t^j.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Reindex the first sum, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{j=1}^k (j \alpha_j - c_{j-1} \alpha_{j-1})t^{j-1} E_{2j} - \alpha_k c_k t^k E_{2k+2} \le 0.
	\end{equation*}
	Since $t \le 1$ and $H(t) \le \bar H$, the remaining terms satisfy
	\begin{equation*}
		F_k' \le  B_k F_k + D_k  \bar{H}, \qquad
		B_k  = \max_{0 \le j \le k} b_j, \qquad
		D_k  = \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j d_j.
	\end{equation*}
	Since $F_k(0) = E_0(0) \le \bar H$, Gronwall's inequality yields
	\begin{equation*}
		F_k(t) \le e^{B_kt} \left( F_k(0) + D_k \bar{H} t \right) \le e^{B_k} (1+D_k)  \bar{H}.
	\end{equation*}
	Finally $\alpha_k t^k E_{2k}(t) \le F_k(t)$ proves \Cref{eq:cascade-bound}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
If $K = 0$, the solution is flat and the assertion is immediate. Otherwise set
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:parabolic-rescaling-B}
		\hat g(s) = K g(K^{-2}s), \qquad 0 \le s \le K^2 \tau \le 1.
	\end{equation}
	Since $J_{cg} = c^{-1} J_g$, it is again a $J$-flow, with $| \mathrm{Rm}_{\hat g} | \le 1$. To see explicitly how the zeroth level controls all higher levels, write 
	\begin{equation*}
		\widehat{\mathcal E}_m(s) := \int_M \left| \nabla_{\hat g(s)}^m \mathrm{Rm}_{\hat g(s)}\right|_{\hat g(s)}^2 dv_{\hat g(s)}.
	\end{equation*}
	In the rescaled flow, \Cref{eq:basic-energy-inequality} reads
	\begin{equation*}
		\widehat{\mathcal E}_m'(s) + a_n \widehat{\mathcal E}_{m+2}(s) \le C_m \widehat{\mathcal E}_0(s).
	\end{equation*}
	Taking $m = 0$, discarding the nonnegative term and applying Gronwall's inequality, we obtain $\widehat{\mathcal E}_0(s) \le e^{C_0s} \widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0) \le e^{C_0}\widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0)$, since $s \le 1$. Consequently, for each even level,
	\begin{equation*}
		\widehat{\mathcal E}_{2j}'(s) + a_n \widehat{\mathcal E}_{2j+2}(s) \le C_{2j} e^{C_0} \widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0).
	\end{equation*}
	Use \Cref{lem:energy-cascade} with $c_j = a_n$, $b_j = 0$ and a constant source, $\sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j s^j \widehat{\mathcal E}_{2j}(s)$ has initial value $\widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0)$ and derivative bounded above by $C_k \widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0)$, thus
	\begin{equation*}
		\widehat{\mathcal E}_{2j}(s) \le C_j \widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0) s^{-j}.
	\end{equation*}
	For odd levels, the first inequality in \Cref{eq:adjacent-Young} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\widehat{\mathcal E}_{2j+1}(s) \le C \left(\widehat{\mathcal E}_{2j}(s) \widehat{\mathcal E}_{2j+2}(s) \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \le C(C_j, C_{j+1}) \widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0) s^{-\frac{2j+1}{2}}.
	\end{equation*}
	By the scaling property, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\widehat{\mathcal E}_m(K^2t) = K^{\frac{n}{2}-m-2} \mathcal E_m^g(t), 
		\qquad 
		\widehat{\mathcal E}_0(0) = K^{\frac{n}{2}-2} \mathcal E_0^g(0).
	\end{equation*}
	Substituting the rescaled estimate at $s = K^2 t$ gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal E_m^g(t) \le C_m \mathcal E_0^g(0) t^{-\frac{m}{2}}.
	\end{equation*}
	Taking square roots proves the stated estimate, with constants depending only on $n,m$.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:tensor-Sobolev}
	Let $(M^4,g)$ be a closed Riemannian manifold satisfying
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:normalized-Sobolev}
		\|u\|_4^2 \le C_S \left( \|\nabla u\|_2^2 + \|u\|_2^2 \right)
	\end{equation}
	for every smooth function $u$. For tensors of fixed rank, there is a constant $C$, depending only on $C_S$ and the rank, such that every smooth tensor $T$ of this rank satisfies
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:tensor-Sobolev}
		\|T\|_\infty \le C \sum_{j=0}^{3} \|\nabla^j T\|_2.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The scalar inequality and Kato's inequality give
	\begin{equation*}
		\|T\|_4^2 \le C_S \left(\|\nabla T\|_2^2 + \|T\|_2^2 \right).
	\end{equation*}
	Applying \Cref{eq:normalized-Sobolev} to $|T|^2$, followed by H\"older's inequality and the preceding estimate for $T$ and $\nabla T$, yields
	\begin{equation*}
		\|T\|_8^4 \le C_S \left( 4 \|T\|_4^2 \|\nabla T\|_4^2 + \|T\|_4^4 \right) \le C \left(\sum_{j=0}^{2} \|\nabla^j T\|_2 \right)^4.
	\end{equation*}
	Apply this estimate to $T$ and $\nabla T$. The multiplicative Sobolev inequality of \cite[Theorem~6.1]{Streets2008}, with both finite exponents equal to eight, and Kato's inequality then give
	\begin{equation*}
		\|T\|_\infty \le C \|T\|_8^{\frac{1}{2}} \left( \|\nabla T\|_8 + \|T\|_8 \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \le C\sum_{j=0}^{3} \|\nabla^j T\|_2.
	\end{equation*}
	The scalar inequalities apply to tensor norms by smooth approximation.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:BBS-pointwise}
	Let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on a closed manifold $M^n$, $n \ge 4$, for $0 \le t \le \tau$, with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose that $|\mathrm{Rm}|_{g(t)} \le K$ on $M \times [0, \tau]$ for some $K > 0$. Fix an integer $m \ge 0$.
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item If $n = 4$, there exists a constant $C_m$, depending only on $m$, such that
		\begin{equation}\label{eq:BBS-pointwise}
			\sup_M | \nabla^m \mathrm{Rm} |_{g(t)} \le C_m \left( K+t^{-\frac{1}{2}} \right)^{1 + \frac{m}{2}}, \qquad 0 < t \le \tau.
		\end{equation}
		\item For any $n > 4$, assume in addition that $\tau \le K^{-2}$ and that there exists a constant $C_S$, which may depend on $m$ but is independent of $t$, such that
		\begin{equation}
			\|T\|_{\infty, g(t)} \le C_S \sum_{i=0}^{k_0} \| \nabla^i T\|_{2, g(t)}, \qquad k_0 = \left\lfloor \frac{n}{2} \right\rfloor + 1,
		\end{equation}
		for every smooth covariant tensor $T$ of rank $m + 4$ and every $0 < t \le \min\{\tau,1\}$. Then there exists a constant $C(n,m)$, depending only on $n,m$, such that
		\begin{equation*}
			\|\nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}\|_{\infty,g(t)} \le C(n,m) C_S \|\mathrm{Rm}_{g_0}\|_{2,g_0} t^{-\frac{m+k_0}{4}}, \qquad 0 < t \le \min\{\tau, 1\}.
		\end{equation*}
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
First suppose $n = 4$. Since $\gamma_4 = 0$, the leading curvature term in \Cref{eq:Rm-evolution} is $- \frac{1}{4} \Delta^2 \mathrm{Rm}$. The time change
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde g(s) = g(4s), \qquad 0 \le s \le \frac{\tau}{4},
	\end{equation*}
	normalizes this coefficient to $-1$, while the metric evolution has the required schematic form $P_{1,2}(\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{2,0}(\mathrm{Rm})$. Thus the rescaled solution satisfies the system in~\cite[Equation~(1.1)]{Streets2013}. Since $M$ is closed, the metrics are complete, and the curvature bound remains $K$. Consequently, \cite[Theorem~1.3]{Streets2013} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_M |\nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}|_{\tilde g(s)} \le C_m \left(K + s^{-\frac{1}{2}} \right)^{1+ \frac{m}{2}}, \qquad 0 < s \le \frac{\tau}{4},
	\end{equation*}
	where the curvature tensor and covariant derivatives are taken with respect to $\tilde g(s)$. Changing back to $t = 4s$ and absorbing the resulting numerical factor into $C_m$ proves \Cref{eq:BBS-pointwise}. This application requires no Sobolev bound.

	Now suppose $ n > 4$. Then $\gamma_n > 0$, and \Cref{eq:Rm-evolution} contains the additional fourth-order term $g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R$. Under the additional restriction $\tau \le  K^{-2}$, we instead use \Cref{theorem:B}. Applying the assumed Sobolev inequality to the covariant tensor $\nabla^m \mathrm{Rm}$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\|\nabla^m\mathrm{Rm}\|_{\infty,g(t)}
			\le C_S \sum_{i=0}^{k_0} \| \nabla^{m+i} \mathrm{Rm} \|_{2,g(t)}
			\le C_S \|\mathrm{Rm}_{g_0} \|_{2,g_0} \sum_{i=0}^{k_0} C_{m+i} t^{-\frac{m+i}{4}}
			\le C(n,m) C_S \| \mathrm{Rm}_{g_0} \|_{2,g_0} t^{- \frac{m+k_0}{4}}
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	for $0 < t \le \min\{\tau, 1\}$, where the last inequality uses $t \le 1$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:weighted-Bochner-identities}
	Let $(M^n,g)$ be a closed Riemannian manifold, and let $0 \le \eta \le 1$ be a smooth cutoff. For integers $j \ge 0$ and $s \ge 2$, the covariant commutator has the schematic form
	\begin{equation*}
		[\Delta, \nabla_a] \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} = \mathrm{Rm} * \nabla^{j+1} \mathrm{Rm} + \nabla \mathrm{Rm} * \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}.
	\end{equation*}
	Moreover,
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M \nabla^b (\eta^s) \left\langle \nabla^a \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla_b \nabla_a \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g = -\frac{1}{2} \int_M \Delta (\eta^s) |\nabla^{j+1} \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:fixed-weight-interpolation}
	Let $T$ be a smooth tensor and let $0 \le \eta \le 1$ be a smooth compactly supported cutoff with $|\nabla \eta| + |\nabla^2 \eta| \le L$. For integers $0 \le l < k$, any $q \ge 2l$ and every $\varepsilon > 0$, one has
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-weight-ladder}
		\begin{split}
			\int_{\{\eta>0\}}\eta^q|\nabla^l T|^2 dv_g
			&\le \varepsilon \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q+2(k-l)} |\nabla^k T|^2 dv_g +  C_\varepsilon\int_{\{\eta>0\}}\eta^{q-2l}|T|^2 dv_g\\
			&\le \varepsilon \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q+2(k-l)} |\nabla^k T|^2 dv_g +  C_\varepsilon \int_{\{\eta>0\}}  |T|^2 dv_g.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation}
	The constant $C_\varepsilon$ depends only on $n,l,k,q,L,\varepsilon$ and the tensor rank.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Suppose $l \ge 1$. Integration by parts and Cauchy--Schwarz give
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\int_{\{\eta>0\}}\eta^q|\nabla^l T|^2 dv_g
			& = - \int_M \eta^q \langle \nabla^{l-1} T, \Delta \nabla^{l-1} T \rangle dv_g -q \int_M \eta^{q-1} \nabla_a \eta \langle \nabla^{l-1} T, \nabla^a \nabla^{l-1} T \rangle dv_g\\
			&\le \sqrt{n} \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q-2} |\nabla^{l-1} T|^2 dv_g \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q+2} |\nabla^{l+1}T|^2 dv_g \right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
			&+ q L \left(\int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q-2} |\nabla^{l-1}T|^2 dv_g \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^q |\nabla^l T|^2 dv_g \right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Young's inequality and absorption yield, for every $\delta > 0$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^q | \nabla^l T |^2 dv_g \le \delta \int_{\{\eta > 0\}} \eta^{q+2} | \nabla^{l+1}T |^2 dv_g + C_\delta \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q-2} | \nabla^{l-1} T |^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	We now use induction on $l$ to remove the last derivative term. For $l = 1$ it is already of order zero. For $l > 1$, apply the inductively obtained estimate between levels $l - 1$ and $l$ with parameter $\theta > 0$:
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q-2} | \nabla^{l-1} T |^2 dv_g \le \theta \int_{\{\eta > 0\}}\eta^q | \nabla^l T |^2 dv_g + C_\theta \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{q - 2l} |T|^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Choose $\theta$ so that $C_\delta \theta \le \frac{1}{2}$ and absorb. This gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{\{\eta > 0\}} \eta^q |\nabla^l T|^2 dv_g \le 2 \delta \int_{\{\eta >0 \}} \eta^{q+2} |\nabla^{l + 1}T|^2 dv_g + C_\delta \int_{\{\eta > 0\}} \eta^{q - 2l} |T|^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Iterating from level $l$ to level $k$, with the weight increased by two at each step, proves the first inequality in \Cref{eq:fixed-weight-ladder}. Since $q - 2l \ge 0$ and $0 \le \eta \le 1$, the second inequality follows. The case $l = 0$ is immediate.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:weighted-Hamilton-interpolation}
	Let $T$ be a smooth tensor and let $0 \le \eta \le 1$ be a smooth compactly supported cutoff with $|\nabla \eta| + |\nabla^2 \eta| \le L$. Suppose $|T| \le K$ on $\{\eta>0\}$. If $k \ge 1$ is an integer and $s \ge 2k$, then for every integer $1 \le i \le k$, one has
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-Hamilton-integral}
		\int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^i T|^{\frac{2k}{i}} dv_g \le C K^{\frac{2k}{i}-2} \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^k T|^2 dv_g + \int_{\{\eta>0\}} |T|^2 dv_g \right).
	\end{equation}
	The constant $C$ depends only on $n,k,s,L$ and the tensor rank.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The case $K = 0$ is immediate. For $K > 0$, the weighted interpolation inequality of Kuwert--Sch\"atzle~\cite[Theorem~5.4]{KuwertSchatzle2002}, in the local form recorded by Jachan~\cite[Lemma~B.22]{Jachan2014}, gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^i T|^{\frac{2k}{i}} dv_g \right)^{\frac{i}{2k}} \le C K^{1-\frac{i}{k}} \left[ \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^k T|^2 dv_g \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} + \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} |T|^2 dv_g \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \right]^{\frac{i}{k}},
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	with the cutoff dependence absorbed into $C$. Raising both sides to the power $\frac{2k}{i}$ and using $(a+b)^2 \le 2 a^2 + 2 b^2$ proves \Cref{eq:weighted-Hamilton-integral}, since $(1-i/k)(2k/i) = 2k/i-2$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:fixed-weight-product}
	Let $T$ be a smooth tensor and let $0 \le \eta \le 1$ be a smooth compactly supported cutoff with $|\nabla \eta| + |\nabla^2 \eta| \le L$. If $k \ge 1$ and $r \ge 2$ are integers, $|T| \le K$ on $\{\eta > 0\}$, $s \ge 2k$, and the integers $0 \le i_1, \ldots , i_r \le k$ satisfy $i_1 + \cdots + i_r =2 k$, then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:fixed-weight-product}
		\left| \int_M \eta^s \nabla^{i_1} T * \cdots * \nabla^{i_r} T dv_g \right| \le C K^{r-2} \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^k T|^2 dv_g + \int_{ \{\eta>0\}} |T|^2 dv_g \right).
	\end{equation}
	The constant $C$ depends only on $n,k,r,s,L$, the tensor rank and the contraction coefficients. It does not depend on a Sobolev constant.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
If $K = 0$, the assertion is immediate. Assume $K > 0$ and bound the undifferentiated factors by $K$. H\"older's inequality and \Cref{lem:weighted-Hamilton-interpolation} yield
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\left| \int_M \eta^s \nabla^{i_1} T * \cdots * \nabla^{i_r} T dv_g \right|
			&\le C\left( \prod_{\{\nu:i_\nu=0\}} K \right) \prod_{\{\nu:i_\nu>0\}}
			\left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^{i_\nu}T|^{\frac{2k}{i_\nu}} dv_g \right)^{\frac{i_\nu}{2k}}\\
			&\le CK^{\sum_{\nu=1}^r (1- \frac{i_\nu}{k})} \left( \int_{\{ \eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^k T|^2 dv_g +\int_{\{\eta>0\}} |T|^2 dv_g \right)^{\sum_{\nu=1}^r \frac{i_\nu}{2k}}\\
			&=C K^{r-2} \left( \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^s |\nabla^k T|^2 dv_g + \int_{\{\eta>0\}} |T|^2 dv_g \right).
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	This proves \Cref{eq:fixed-weight-product}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:local-energy}
	Let $g(t)$ be a smooth $J$-flow in dimension $n \ge 4$. Suppose $| \mathrm{Rm} | \le K$ on $\{\eta > 0\} \times [0, \tau]$, where $\eta$ is smooth, compactly supported and time independent, $0 \le \eta \le 1$, and $|\nabla \eta| + |\nabla^2 \eta| \le L$ throughout this interval. For $s \ge 2j + 4$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-energy-inequality}
		\frac{d}{dt} E_{j,s}^{\eta} + c_j E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} \le C(n,j,s,L) K^2 E_{j,s}^{\eta} + C(n,j,s,L)(1 + K^2) H_\eta(t),
	\end{equation}
	where $c_j > 0$ depends only on $n, j$ and $H_\eta(t) = \int_{\{\eta>0\}} |\mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g$.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
All integrals and covariant derivatives are taken with respect to $g(t)$, and the constants depend on $n, j, s, L$ unless otherwise indicated. Since $\eta$ is time independent, differentiating the energy and then using \Cref{eq:Rm-derivative-evolution} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\frac{d}{dt} E_{j,s}^{\eta}
			&= -2a_n \int_M \eta^s \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \Delta^2 \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g - \gamma_n \int_M \eta^s \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla^j(g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R) \right\rangle dv_g\\
			&\quad + \int_M \eta^s \left( P_{3,2j+2}(\mathrm{Rm}) + P_{4,2j}(\mathrm{Rm}) \right) dv_g.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	We first treat the biharmonic term. Integration by parts and \Cref{lem:weighted-Bochner-identities} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			&-2 a_n \int_M \eta^s \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm},\Delta^2 \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g\\
			=& -2 a_n \int_M \eta^s |\Delta \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g - 4 a_n \int_M \nabla^a(\eta^s) \left\langle \nabla_a \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \Delta \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g\\
			&- 2 a_n \int_M \Delta(\eta^s) \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \Delta \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g\\
			=&-2 a_n E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + a_n \int_M \Delta(\eta^s) |\nabla^{j+1} \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g - 2a_n \int_M \nabla^a(\eta^s) \left\langle \nabla_a \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm},\Delta \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g \\
			&-2 a_n \int_M \Delta(\eta^s) \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm},\Delta \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g - 2a_n \int_M \eta^s \left\langle \nabla^a \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm},[\Delta, \nabla_a] \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \right\rangle dv_g\\
			\le& - (2a_n - \delta) E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + \left| \int_M \eta^s P_{3,2j+2}(\mathrm{Rm})dv_g \right| + C_\delta \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{s-2} |\nabla^{j+1} \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g + C_\delta \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{s-4} |\nabla^j\mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g\\
			\le& - (2a_n - \delta) E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + C_\delta\theta E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + \left| \int_M \eta^s P_{3,2j+2}(\mathrm{Rm})dv_g \right| + C_\delta C_\theta H_\eta(t)\\
			\le& - (2a_n - \varepsilon) E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + \left| \int_M \eta^s P_{3,2j+2}(\mathrm{Rm})dv_g \right| + C_\varepsilon H_\eta(t).
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	The first inequality follows from $|\nabla(\eta^s)|\le C\eta^{s-1}$, $|\nabla^2 (\eta^s)| \le C \eta^{s-2}$ and Young's inequality. For any prescribed $\varepsilon > 0$, first choose $0 < \delta \le \varepsilon / 2$ and then apply \Cref{eq:fixed-weight-ladder} to the two lower-weight integrals, from levels $j+1$ and $j$ to $j+2$, with weights $s-2$ and $s-4$, respectively, choosing the interpolation parameters so that the sum of these integrals is bounded by $\theta E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + C_\theta H_\eta(t)$. Since $s \ge 2j + 4$, the resulting zeroth-order weights are nonnegative, when $j = 0$, the second integral is bounded directly by $H_\eta(t)$. Finally, choose $\theta > 0$ so that $C_\delta\theta\le\varepsilon/2$. Then $\delta+C_\delta \theta \le \varepsilon$, and absorbing $C_\delta C_\theta$ into $C_\varepsilon$ gives the last inequality. The remaining cubic integral is estimated below together with the other curvature polynomial terms.

	We next estimate the curvature polynomial terms. The weighted interpolation estimates \cite[Lemmas~10.1--10.3]{Streets2008}, together with \Cref{eq:fixed-weight-ladder,eq:fixed-weight-product}, give, for every $\varepsilon > 0$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-polynomial-bound}
		\begin{split}
			\left| \int_M \eta^s P_{3, 2j + 2} (\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g \right| + \left| \int_M \eta^s P_{4,2 j}(\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g \right| \le \varepsilon E_{j + 2,s}^{\eta} + C_\varepsilon K^2 E_{j,s}^{\eta} + C_\varepsilon(1 + K^2) H_\eta(t).
		\end{split}
	\end{equation}
	This estimate also covers a cubic term with derivative orders $j + 2, a, b$, where $a+b=j$, Cauchy--Schwarz and \Cref{eq:fixed-weight-product} with $k = j$, $r = 4$ applied to the squared product of the last two factors give
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M \eta^s |\nabla^{j+2} \mathrm{Rm}| |\nabla^a \mathrm{Rm}| |\nabla^b \mathrm{Rm}| dv_g \le C K \left( E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} \right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \left( E_{j,s}^{\eta} + H_\eta(t) \right)^{\frac{1}{2}},
	\end{equation*}
	and Young's inequality applies. When $j = 0$, the same bound follows directly from $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le K$.

	Applying these bounds to the differentiated energy and choosing the Young parameters small enough, as in \cite[Corollary~5.2]{Streets2008}, gives
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-energy-before-scalar}
		\frac{d}{dt} E_{j,s}^{\eta} + \frac{a_n}{2} E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} \le -\gamma_n \int_M \eta^s \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla^j (g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R) \right\rangle dv_g + C K^2 E_{j,s}^{\eta} + C(1+K^2) H_\eta(t).
	\end{equation}

	It remains to estimate the scalar-curvature term. As in the proof of \Cref{eq:gamma-perfect-square}, commuting the Hessian derivatives past $\nabla^j$ and integrating by parts twice gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M \eta^s \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla^j (g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R) \right\rangle dv_g = 4 \int_M \left\langle \operatorname{div}^2 (\eta^s \nabla^j \mathrm{Ric}), \nabla^j \Delta R \right\rangle dv_g + \int_M \eta^s P_{3,2j+2} (\mathrm{Rm}) dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	The product rule and the contracted Bianchi identity yield
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{div}^2 (\eta^s \nabla^j \mathrm{Ric}) = \frac{1}{2} \eta^s \nabla^j \Delta R + \eta^s P_{2,j}(\mathrm{Rm}) + \nabla(\eta^s) * \nabla^{j+1} \mathrm{Ric} + \nabla^2 (\eta^s) * \nabla^j \mathrm{Ric}.
	\end{equation*}
	The curvature commutators vanish when $j = 0$. In general, after pairing with $\nabla^j \Delta R$, all cubic terms have total derivative order $2j + 2$ and no factor has more than $j + 2$ derivatives, so \Cref{eq:weighted-polynomial-bound} applies. For the remaining terms, $|\nabla(\eta^s)| \le C \eta^{s-1}$, $|\nabla^2 (\eta^s)| \le C \eta^{s-2}$ and $|\nabla^j \Delta R| \le C(n) |\nabla^{j+2}\mathrm{Rm}|$ give
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			&C \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{s-1} |\nabla^{j+1} \mathrm{Rm}| |\nabla^{j+2} \mathrm{Rm}| dv_g + C \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{s-2} |\nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}| |\nabla^{j+2} \mathrm{Rm}| dv_g\\
			\le& \delta E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + C_\delta \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{s-2} |\nabla^{j+1} \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g + C_\delta \int_{\{\eta>0\}} \eta^{s-4} |\nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g\\
			\le& \varepsilon E_{j+2,s}^{\eta}  + C_\varepsilon H_\eta(t).
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Here we first choose $\delta$ and then apply \Cref{eq:fixed-weight-ladder} from levels $j+1$ and $j$ to $j+2$, with weights $s-2$ and $s-4$, respectively. When $j = 0$, the second lower-weight integral is bounded directly by $H_\eta(t)$. Since $\gamma_n \ge 0$, it follows that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-scalar-bound}
		-\gamma_n \int_M \eta^s \left\langle \nabla^j \mathrm{Rm}, \nabla^j(g \odot \nabla^2 \Delta R) \right\rangle dv_g \le - 2 \gamma_n \int_M \eta^s |\nabla^j \Delta R|^2 dv_g + \varepsilon E_{j+2,s}^{\eta} + C_\varepsilon K^2 E_{j,s}^{\eta} + C_\varepsilon(1+K^2) H_\eta(t).
	\end{equation}
	Combining \Cref{eq:local-energy-before-scalar,eq:weighted-scalar-bound}, taking $\varepsilon = a_n / 4$ and discarding the nonpositive terms proves \Cref{eq:local-energy-inequality} with $c_j = a_n / 4$. In dimension four the calculation is simpler because $\gamma_4 = 0$, so the scalar-curvature principal term is absent.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:weighted-interpolation}
	Let $T$ be a smooth tensor and let $0 \le \phi \le 1$ be a smooth compactly supported cutoff with $|\nabla \phi| \le L$. Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:interpolation-recursion}
		\int_M \phi^2 |\nabla T|^2 dv_g \le C(n) \|T\|_{L^2(\{\phi > 0\})} \|\nabla^2T\|_{L^2(\{\phi>0\})} + C(n) L^2 \|T\|_{L^2(\{\phi > 0\})}^2.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Compact support permits the integration by parts
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M \phi^2 |\nabla T|^2 dv_g = - \int_M \phi^2 \langle T, \Delta T \rangle d v_g - 2 \int_M \phi \langle T, \nabla_{\nabla \phi} T \rangle dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Using $|\Delta T| \le \sqrt{n} |\nabla^2T|$ in the first integral and Young's inequality in the second gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M\phi^2|\nabla T|^2 dv_g \le \sqrt{n} \|T\|_{L^2(\{\phi>0\})} \|\nabla^2 T \|_{L^2(\{\phi>0\})} + \frac{1}{2} \int_M \phi^2 |\nabla T|^2 dv_g + 2 L^2 \|T\|_{L^2(\{\phi>0\})}^2.
	\end{equation*}
	Moving the middle term to the left proves the assertion. The computation uses no commutation of covariant derivatives.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
We apply the same time-weighted energy argument to the localized energies.
	Fix $t_* \le \min\{\tau, 1\}$ and set
	\begin{equation*}
		H_*: = \sup_{0 \le t \le t_*} \int_{B_{2r}(p)} |\mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_{g(t)}.
	\end{equation*}
	Choose the cutoff $\Phi$ from \Cref{eq:cutoff-estimate}. To estimate all even levels up to $2k$, choose a single integer $s \ge 4k+4$. \Cref{thm:local-energy} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{d}{dt} E_{2j,s}^{\Phi}(t) + c_{2j} E_{2j+2,s}^{\Phi}(t) \le b_j E_{2j,s}^{\Phi}(t) + d_j H_*, \qquad 0 \le j \le k,
	\end{equation*}
	where $b_j = C(n,2j,s,L) K^2$ and $d_j = C(n,2j,s,L) (1+K^2)$. Here we used $H_\Phi(t)\le H_*$ for $0 \le t \le t_*$, since $\{\Phi>0\} \subset B_{2r}(p)$. The equality of the weights at adjacent levels is essential in the following calculation. Set 
	\begin{equation*}
		G_k(t) = \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j t^j E_{2j,s}^{\Phi}(t),
	\end{equation*}
	where $\alpha_0 = 1$ and $j \alpha_j \le \frac{1}{2}c_{2j-2} \alpha_{j-1}$. Differentiating and reindexing, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			G_k'(t)
			\le& \sum_{j=1}^k(j \alpha_j - c_{2j-2} \alpha_{j-1}) t^{j-1} E_{2j,s}^{\Phi}(t) - c_{2k} \alpha_k t^k E_{2k+2,s}^{\Phi}(t) + \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j b_j t^j E_{2j,s}^{\Phi}(t) + H_* \sum_{j=0}^k \alpha_j d_j t^j\\
			\le& C G_k(t) + C H_*.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Since $G_k(0) = E_{0,s}^{\Phi}(0) \le H_*$ and $t_* \le1$, integration gives $G_k (t_*) \le C(n,k,K,L) H_*$. In particular,
	\begin{equation*}
		E_{2k,s}^{\Phi}(t_*) \le C(n,k,K,L) H_* t_*^{-k}.
	\end{equation*}
	Because $\Phi = 1$ on $\{\phi > 0\}$, this bounds the unweighted even-level energy on that entire set. 

	For $m = 2k+1$, obtain the even estimates at levels $2k$ and $2k + 2$ by choosing $s \ge 4k + 8$. Apply \Cref{eq:interpolation-recursion} to $T = \nabla^{2k} \mathrm{Rm}$. Since $\phi = 1$ on $B_r(p)$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{B_r(p)} |\nabla^{2k+1} \mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_{g(t_*)} \le C H_* \left(t_*^{-\frac{2k+1}{2}} + L^2 t_*^{-k} \right) \le C(n,k,K,L) H_* t_*^{ -\frac{2k+1}{2} }.
	\end{equation*}
	This proves \Cref{eq:local-BBS} for every $m$, since $t_*$ was arbitrary.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rmk:local-higher-dim}
	For the scaled version, assume $r |\nabla f| + r^2 |\nabla^2f| \le L$ for both cutoffs and use $\hat g(s) = r^{-2} g(r^4 s)$. Then \Cref{eq:local-BBS} holds for $0 < t \le \min\{\tau, r^4\}$ with $C = C(n,m,K r^2,L)$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:curvature-continuation}
	If a smooth $J$-flow on $[0 , T )$, $T < \infty$, has bounded curvature, it extends smoothly past $T$. In particular a finite maximal time implies $\limsup_{t \rightarrow T}\|\mathrm{Rm}_{g(t)}\|_\infty = \infty$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Fix $t_1 \in (0,T)$. By \Cref{cor:BBS-pointwise}, all covariant curvature derivatives are uniformly bounded for $t \in [t_1,T)$. Since $h = \partial_t g = -2J$ is a universal sum of $\nabla^2 \mathrm{Rm} * g$ and $\mathrm{Rm} * \mathrm{Rm}$, all $\nabla^j h$ are bounded there, in particular $|h|_g \le C$. Integrating $|\partial_t \log g(v,v)| \le C$ gives
	\begin{equation*}
		e^{-C(T - t_1)} g(t_1) \le g(t) \le e^{C(T - t_1)} g(t_1).
	\end{equation*}
	Thus the metrics remain positive and uniformly equivalent on this finite interval.

	These intrinsic estimates also control the metrics in fixed coordinates. The connection variation formula gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_t \left(\nabla^{g(t)} - \nabla^{g(t_1)} \right)^k_{ij} = \frac{1}{2} g^{kl} (\nabla_i h_{jl} + \nabla_j h_{il} - \nabla_l h_{ij}).
	\end{equation*}
	The connection difference vanishes at $t = t_1$, so integration, metric equivalence and the bound on $\nabla h$ give a uniform bound for $\nabla^{g(t)} - \nabla^{g(t_1)}$ in the fixed metric $g(t_1)$. Since $\nabla^{g(t)} g(t) =0$, the identity
	\begin{equation*}
		\nabla^{g(t_1)}g(t) = \left(\nabla^{g(t)} - \nabla^{g(t_1)} \right) * g(t)
	\end{equation*}
	then bounds the first derivatives of $g(t)$ with respect to the fixed connection. 
	
	At each higher order, apply $(\nabla^{g(t_1)})^j$ to the connection variation formula and express the fixed-connection derivatives of $h$ using $\nabla^{g(t)}$ and the connection difference. The resulting equation involves bounded covariant derivatives of $h$, lower fixed-connection derivatives of $\nabla^{g(t)} - \nabla^{g(t_1)}$, and terms at most linear in $(\nabla^{g(t_1)})^j (\nabla^{g(t)} - \nabla^{g(t_1)})$. Gronwall's inequality on the finite interval and the preceding identity therefore bound all fixed-connection derivatives of the connection difference and of $g(t)$. Consequently $(\nabla^{g(t_1)})^j h$ is uniformly bounded for every $j$.

	It follows from $g(t) - g(s) = \int_s^t h(u) du$ that $g(t)$ is Cauchy in every fixed $C^j$ norm as $t \rightarrow T$. Its limit $g(T)$ is smooth and positive definite. Apply \Cref{theorem:A} to $g(T)$ and join the solutions, the equation supplies matching time derivatives, and uniqueness gives a smooth extension.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:integral-pointwise}
	Let $g(t)$ be a smooth $J$-flow on a closed four-manifold for $0 \le t \le1$, with $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le 1$ throughout this interval. If \Cref{eq:normalized-Sobolev} holds with respect to $g(1)$, then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:integral-pointwise}
		\|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{\infty, g(1)} \le C(C_S) \|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{2,g(0)}.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Apply \Cref{eq:tensor-Sobolev} to the curvature tensor at time one and \Cref{theorem:B} with $K = \tau = 1$ at derivative orders $0,1,2,3$. This gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\| \mathrm{Rm} \|_{\infty,g(1)} \le C(C_S) \sum_{j=0}^{3} \|\nabla^j \mathrm{Rm} \|_{2,g(1)} \le C(C_S) \| \mathrm{Rm} \|_{2,g(0)}.
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Let $C(C_S)$ be the constant in \Cref{eq:integral-pointwise} and choose $\varepsilon(C_S) = (4C(C_S)^2)^{-1}$. Suppose that curvature is unbounded on the maximal interval, whether that interval is finite or infinite. Choose $K_i \to \infty$, greater than the initial curvature maximum, and let $t_i$ be the first time at which $\|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{\infty, g(t_i)} = K_i$. The first-time choice gives $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le K_i$ on $[0,t_i]$. Since the solution is smooth on an initial interval, the $t_i$ are bounded away from zero for large $i$. Thus $K_i^2 t_i \ge 1$ and $K_i r_0^2 \ge 1$ for all sufficiently large $i$.

	Rescale the final interval of length $K_i^{-2}$ by setting
	\begin{equation*}
		\hat g_i(s) = K_i g \left(t_i + K_i^{-2}(s-1) \right), \qquad 0 \le s \le 1.
	\end{equation*}
	After scaling, this is again a solution, with $|\mathrm{Rm}_{\hat g_i(s)}| \le 1$ throughout and $\|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{\infty, \hat g_i(1)} = 1$. At its final time, \Cref{eq:uniform-Sobolev-D} becomes
	\begin{equation*}
		\|u\|_{4, \hat g_i(1)}^2
		\le C_S \left( \|\nabla u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2 + (K_i r_0^2)^{-1} \|u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2 \right)\\
		\le C_S \left( \|\nabla u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2 + \|u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2 \right).
	\end{equation*}
	The four-dimensional curvature energy is unchanged by constant metric rescaling, so the assumed energy bound at the beginning of this interval gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{2, \hat g_i(0)}^2 = \|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{2,g(t_i - K_i^{-2})}^2 < \varepsilon(C_S).
	\end{equation*}
	Applying \Cref{eq:integral-pointwise} to $\hat g_i$ now yields
	\begin{equation*}
		1 = \|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{\infty, \hat g_i(1)} \le C(C_S) \|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{2,\hat g_i(0)} < C(C_S) \sqrt{\varepsilon(C_S)} = \frac{1}{2},
	\end{equation*}
	a contradiction. Curvature is therefore uniformly bounded on the whole maximal interval. If $T_{\max} < \infty$, \Cref{prop:curvature-continuation} extends the solution past $T_{\max}$, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rmk:continuation-scope}
	The proof uses the assumed curvature-energy bound at the earlier times $t_i - K_i^{-2}$ and the Sobolev inequality at the first maximum times $t_i$. The constant in \Cref{eq:integral-pointwise} depends only on $C_S$, so the threshold is independent of $g_0$ and $r_0$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:J-evolution}
	Along a smooth $J$-flow on a closed four-manifold, the $J$-tensor satisfies
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-tensor-evolution}
		\partial_t J = - \frac{1}{4} \Delta^2 J + \mathrm{Rm} * \nabla^2 J + \nabla \mathrm{Rm} * \nabla J + \nabla^2 \mathrm{Rm} * J + \mathrm{Rm} * \mathrm{Rm} * J.
	\end{equation}
	Each schematic term denotes a finite sum of universal contractions.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
By \Cref{prop:trace-J,prop:J-Schur},
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{tr}_g J = Q, \qquad \delta_g J = - \frac{1}{4} dQ, \qquad \delta_g^2 J = \frac{1}{4} \Delta Q.
	\end{equation*}
	Substituting $\partial_t g = -2 J$ into \Cref{eq:Ric-linearization,eq:R-linearization} therefore gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_t \mathrm{Ric} = \Delta J + \frac{1}{2} \nabla^2 Q + \mathrm{Rm} * J,
		\qquad
		\partial_t R = \frac{3}{2} \Delta Q + 2 \langle \mathrm{Ric}, J \rangle.
	\end{equation*}
	Differentiate \Cref{eq:J-dim4-full}. The terms in which the time derivative falls on $\mathrm{Ric}$ or $R$ inside their second derivatives have fourth-order part
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{12} \nabla^2 \left( \frac{3}{2} \Delta Q \right) -\frac{1}{4} \Delta\left( \Delta J + \frac{1}{2} \nabla^2 Q \right) = -\frac{1}{4} \Delta^2 J + \frac{1}{8}(\nabla^2 \Delta Q - \Delta \nabla^2 Q).
	\end{equation*}
	The commutator satisfies
	\begin{equation*}
		[\Delta, \nabla^2] Q = \mathrm{Rm} * \nabla^2 Q + \nabla \mathrm{Rm} * \nabla Q.
	\end{equation*}
	Since $\nabla g = 0$ and $Q = \operatorname{tr}_g J$, these are contractions of $\mathrm{Rm} * \nabla^2 J$ and $\nabla \mathrm{Rm} * \nabla J$. The variations of the differential operators contribute
	\begin{equation*}
		(\partial_t \Delta) \mathrm{Ric} = J * \nabla^2 \mathrm{Rm} + \nabla J * \nabla \mathrm{Rm} + \nabla^2 J * \mathrm{Rm}, \qquad (\partial_t \nabla^2) R = \nabla J * \nabla R.
	\end{equation*}
	Finally, the variation of each quadratic curvature term is of type $\mathrm{Rm} * \nabla^2 J+ \mathrm{Rm} * \mathrm{Rm} * J$, and differentiating the lower-order terms in $\partial_t \mathrm{Ric}$ and $\partial_t R$ gives the remaining contractions in \Cref{eq:J-tensor-evolution}. In particular, every term contains $J$ or one of its derivatives.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:J-energy}
	Let $(M^4, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian four-manifold, and let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^4 \times [0,\infty)$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le K$. For every $t_0 > 0$ and integer $m \ge 0$, there is $C_m = C(m,K,t_0)$ such that, for $t\ge t_0$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-energy}
		\frac d{dt} \| \nabla^m J \|_2^2 + \frac{1}{8} \| \nabla^{m+2} J \|_2^2 \le C_m \|J\|_2^2.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
By \Cref{cor:BBS-pointwise} and \Cref{eq:J-dim4-full}, all covariant derivatives of $\mathrm{Rm}$ and $J$ are uniformly bounded for $t \ge t_0$. Commuting $\nabla^m$ through \Cref{eq:J-tensor-evolution} produces terms with at most $m + 2$ derivatives of $J$ and uniformly bounded curvature coefficients. The time commutator has the form
	\begin{equation*}
		[\partial_t, \nabla^m] J = \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} \nabla^{i+1} J * \nabla^{m-1-i} J,
	\end{equation*}
	with an empty sum when $m = 0$. Bound the first factor of each summand using the preceding uniform estimates. It follows that
	\begin{equation*}
		\left| \partial_t \nabla^m J + \frac{1}{4} \Delta^2 \nabla^m J \right| \le C_m \sum_{\ell = 0}^{m+2} |\nabla^\ell J|.
	\end{equation*}
	The changing contractions and volume element contribute at most $C_m \|\nabla^m J\|_2^2$. Differentiation and integration by parts thus give
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{d}{dt} \| \nabla^m J \|_2^2 + \frac{1}{2} \| \Delta \nabla^m J\|_2^2 \le C_m\|\nabla^m J \|_2 \sum_{\ell=0}^{m+2} \| \nabla^\ell J \|_2.
	\end{equation*}
	The unweighted Bochner calculation, using the uniform bounds on $\mathrm{Rm}$ and $\nabla \mathrm{Rm}$, yields
	\begin{equation*}
		\|\Delta \nabla^m J\|_2^2 \ge \frac{1}{2} \|\nabla^{m+2} J \|_2^2 - C_m \left( \|\nabla^{m+1} J \|_2^2 + \|\nabla^m J \|_2^2 \right).
	\end{equation*}
	Apply Young's inequality to the highest-order factor on the right of the energy estimate. All remaining positive derivative levels are controlled by covariant $L^2$ interpolation:
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{\ell=1}^{m+1} \|\nabla^\ell J \|_2^2 \le \varepsilon\| \nabla^{m+2} J \|_2^2 + C_{m, \varepsilon} \| J \|_2^2.
	\end{equation*}
	This follows by iterating integration by parts, starting from $\|\nabla^\ell J\|_2^2 \le \sqrt{n} \|\nabla^{\ell-1} J \|_2 \|\nabla^{\ell+1} J \|_2$. Choosing the Young and interpolation parameters sufficiently small proves \Cref{eq:J-energy}. Every other term retains a factor of a derivative of $J$ in each of its two $L^2$ factors, so no additive constant or volume term is introduced.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:J-smoothing}
	Let $(M^4, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian four-manifold, and let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^4 \times [0, \infty)$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le K$ and that \Cref{eq:uniform-Sobolev-D} holds with time-independent constants $C_S,r_0$. For every $t_0 > 0$ and integer $m \ge 0$, there is $C_m = C(m,K,t_0,C_S,r_0)$ such that, for every $t \ge t_0$ and $0 < \sigma \le 1$, we have
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-smoothing}
		\|\nabla^m J \|_{2,g(t + \sigma)} \le C_m \sigma^{-\frac{m}{4}} \| J \|_{2,g(t)}, 
	\end{equation}
	and
	\begin{equation*}
		\| \nabla^m J \|_{\infty,g(t+1)} \le C_m \| J \|_{2,g(t)}.
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Fix $t \ge t_0$. The case $m = 0$ of \Cref{eq:J-energy} and Gronwall's inequality give
	\begin{equation*}
		\|J\|_{2, g(t+\sigma)}^2 \le e^{C_0 \sigma} \| J \|_{2,g(t)}^2, \qquad 0 \le \sigma \le 1.
	\end{equation*}
	Apply \Cref{lem:energy-cascade} on this shifted time interval to the energies $\| \nabla^{2j} J \|_{2, g(t + \sigma)}^2$, with coefficient $1/8$ and source bounded by $e^{C_0} \|J\|_{2,g(t)}^2$. This gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\|\nabla^{2j} J \|_{2,g(t+\sigma)}^2 \le C_j\sigma^{-j} \| J \|_{2,g(t)}^2.
	\end{equation*}
	For odd orders, integration by parts gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\|\nabla^{2j+1} J\|_2^2 \le \sqrt{n} \|\nabla^{2j} J\|_2 \| \nabla^{2j+2} J \|_2.
	\end{equation*}
	Substitution proves the first estimate in \Cref{eq:J-smoothing} for every $m$. The scalar Sobolev hypothesis implies the normalized inequality in \Cref{lem:tensor-Sobolev} with a constant depending only on $C_S, r_0$. Apply that lemma to $\nabla^m J$ at time $t + 1$ and use the first estimate at orders $m, m+1, m+2, m+3$ to obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		\|\nabla^m J \|_{\infty, g(t+1)} \le C_m \sum_{\ell=0}^3 \|\nabla^{m + \ell} J \|_{2,g(t+1)} \le C_m \|J\|_{2,g(t)}.
	\end{equation*}
	All constants are uniform in $t\ge t_0$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Fix $t_0>0$. The curvature bound and uniform Sobolev inequality allow us to apply \Cref{prop:J-smoothing} for $t\ge t_0$. Integrating the second estimate in \Cref{eq:J-smoothing} and using \Cref{eq:J-time-integrability} yields, for every $m\ge0$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-integrable-derivatives}
		\int_{t_0+1}^\infty \|\nabla^m J \|_{\infty,g(t)} dt \le C_m \int_{t_0}^\infty \|J\|_{2,g(t)} dt < \infty.
	\end{equation}
	Fix $T=t_0+1$. For a nonzero fixed tangent vector $v$, the flow equation gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\left| \partial_t \log g(t)(v,v) \right| \le 2 \|J\|_{\infty,g(t)}.
	\end{equation*}
	Consequently there is a time-independent constant $\Lambda<\infty$ such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-uniform-metric-equivalence}
		\Lambda^{-1} g(T) \le g(t) \le \Lambda g(T), \qquad t \ge T.
	\end{equation}

	We next control derivatives relative to the fixed connection $\nabla^{g(T)}$. The connection variation formula reads
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_t \left(\nabla^{g(t)} - \nabla^{g(T)} \right)^k_{ij} = -g^{kl} \left(\nabla_i J_{jl} + \nabla_j J_{il} - \nabla_l J_{ij}\right).
	\end{equation*}
	By \Cref{eq:J-uniform-metric-equivalence,eq:J-integrable-derivatives}, its right-hand side is integrable in the fixed supremum norm.
