The connection difference, initially zero at $T$, is therefore uniformly bounded. For higher orders, suppose its fixed derivatives of orders less than $q$ are uniformly bounded. Expanding $(\nabla^{g(T)})^q(g^{-1} * \nabla J)$ in terms of $\nabla^{g(t)}$ introduces only these lower derivatives of the connection difference and covariant derivatives $\nabla^\ell J$ with $1 \le \ell \le q+1$. Hence
	\begin{equation*}
		\left\|\partial_t(\nabla^{g(T)})^q \left( \nabla^{g(t)} - \nabla^{g(T)} \right) \right\|_{\infty,g(T)} \le C_q\sum_{\ell=1}^{q+1} \|\nabla^\ell J\|_{\infty,g(t)}.
	\end{equation*}
	The right-hand side is integrable, so induction bounds every fixed derivative of the connection difference. Converting derivatives of $J$ between the two connections now gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\|(\nabla^{g(T)})^m J \|_{\infty, g(T)}
		\le C_m \sum_{\ell=0}^m \|\nabla^\ell J\|_{\infty, g(t)}.
	\end{equation*}
	Thus every fixed $C^m$ norm of $J$ is integrable in time. For $t_2 \ge t_1 \ge T$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\|g(t_2) - g(t_1)\|_{C^m(g(T))}
		\le 2 \int_{t_1}^{t_2} \|J_{g(t)}\|_{C^m(g(T))} dt
		\longrightarrow 0
	\end{equation*}
	as $t_1 \to \infty$. It follows that the entire family converges smoothly to a metric $g_\infty$, which is positive definite by \Cref{eq:J-uniform-metric-equivalence}. Smooth convergence also implies $\|J_{g(t)}\|_{2,g(t)} \to \|J_{g_\infty}\|_{2,g_\infty}$. A positive limit contradicts \Cref{eq:J-time-integrability}, so $J_{g_\infty} = 0$. Norms defined by any other fixed smooth background metric are equivalent to those defined by $g(T)$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:first-variation}
	Let $h \in S_2(M)$ be a symmetric $2$-tensor. Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-variation-1}
		D \mathcal{J}_g \cdot h = \int_M \Gamma_g h dv_g + \frac{1}{2} \int_M Q_g \operatorname{tr}_g h dv_g ,
	\end{equation}
	and, using the formal adjoint identity and $J_g = -\frac{1}{2} \Gamma_g^*1$, this can be rewritten as
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-variation-2}
		D \mathcal{J}_g \cdot h = \int_M \langle \Gamma_g^*1 + \frac{1}{2} Q_g g, h \rangle_g dv_g = \int_M \langle - 2 J_g + \frac{1}{2} Q_g g, h \rangle_g dv_g .
	\end{equation}
	Consequently the $L^2$-gradient of $\mathcal{J}$ with respect to the standard $L^2$-metric on symmetric $2$-tensors is
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gradient-J}
		\mathrm{grad} \mathcal{J} (g) = -2 J_g + \frac{1}{2} Q_g g .
	\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Let $h \in S_2(M)$. The total $Q$-curvature is $\mathcal J(g) = \int_M Q_g dv_g$. Differentiating under the integral sign gives
	\begin{equation*}
		D \mathcal J_g \cdot h = \int_M (D Q_g \cdot h) dv_g + \int_M Q_g (D dv_g \cdot h).
	\end{equation*}
	By definition of the linearization, $D Q_g \cdot h = \Gamma_g h$. The standard variational formula for the volume element is
	\begin{equation*}
		D dv_g \cdot h = \frac{1}{2} (\operatorname{tr}_gh) dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Substituting these two identities yields \Cref{eq:first-variation-1}.

	To pass to \Cref{eq:first-variation-2}, recall that the $L^2$-inner product on symmetric $2$-tensors is $\int_M \langle u,v \rangle_g dv_g$, and that the formal adjoint satisfies
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M (\Gamma_g h) dv_g = \int_M \langle \Gamma_g^*1, h \rangle_g dv_g .
	\end{equation*}
	Therefore
	\begin{equation*}
		D \mathcal J_g \cdot h = \int_M \left\langle \Gamma_g^*1 + \frac{1}{2} Q_g g, h \right\rangle_g dv_g .
	\end{equation*}
	Using $J_g = -\frac{1}{2} \Gamma_g^*1$ we obtain \Cref{eq:first-variation-2} and hence \Cref{eq:gradient-J}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:Q-evolution}
	Let $g(t)$ be a solution of the $J$-flow $\frac{\partial}{\partial t} g = -2 J_g$ on $M \times [0,T)$. Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Q-evolution}
		\frac{d}{dt} \mathcal{J} (g(t)) = 4 \int_M |J_{g(t)}|_{g(t)}^2 dv_{g(t)} - \int_M Q_{g(t)}^2 dv_{g(t)} .
	\end{equation}
	Equivalently,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Q-evolution-rewritten}
		\frac{d}{dt} \mathcal{J} (g(t)) = 4 \int_M \left|J_{g(t)} - \frac{1}{n} Q_{g(t)}g(t) \right|_{g(t)}^2 dv_{g(t)} - \frac{n-4}{n} \int_M Q_{g(t)}^2 dv_{g(t)} .
	\end{equation}
	In particular, when $n = 4$, the total $Q$-curvature is non-decreasing along the $J$-flow.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Since $\mathcal{J}(g) = \int_M Q dv_g$, the product rule gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{d}{dt} \mathcal{J} (g) = \int_M \frac{\partial Q}{\partial t} dv_g + \int_M Q \frac{\partial}{\partial t} dv_g .
	\end{equation*}
	The first term is computed by linearizing the $Q$-curvature along the flow:
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\partial Q}{\partial t} = D Q_g \cdot \left(\frac{\partial g}{\partial t} \right) = \Gamma_g( -2J) .
	\end{equation*}
	Using the defining relation $J = -\frac{1}{2} \Gamma_g^*1$, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M \Gamma_g (-2J) dv_g = \int_M \langle \Gamma_g^*1, -2J \rangle_g dv_g   = \int_M \langle - 2J, -2J \rangle_g dv_g  = 4 \int_M |J|_g^2 dv_g .
	\end{equation*}
	For the volume term we use the standard variational formula
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\partial}{\partial t} dv_g = \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}_g \left(\frac{\partial g}{\partial t} \right) dv_g = \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}_g( -2J) dv_g = -Q dv_g .
	\end{equation*}
	Consequently,
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M Q \frac{\partial}{\partial t} dv_g = -\int_M Q^2 dv_g .
	\end{equation*}
	Adding the two contributions gives \Cref{eq:Q-evolution}. To obtain \Cref{eq:Q-evolution-rewritten}, we decompose $J$ into its trace and traceless parts. Because $\operatorname{tr}_g J = Q$,
	\begin{equation*}
		J = \frac{1}{n} Q g + \left(J - \frac{1}{n} Q g \right), \qquad \left|J - \frac{1}{n} Q g \right|_g^2 = |J|_g^2 - \frac{1}{n} Q^2 .
	\end{equation*}
	Therefore
	\begin{equation*}
		4|J|_g^2 - Q^2 = 4 \left|J - \frac{1}{n} Q g \right|_g^2 + \frac{4}{n} Q^2 - Q^2 = 4 \left|J - \frac{1}{n} Q g \right|_g^2 - \frac{n-4}{n} Q^2 .
	\end{equation*}
	Integrating over $M$ yields \Cref{eq:Q-evolution-rewritten}. When $n = 4$ the last term vanishes, so $\frac{d}{dt} \mathcal J(g) \ge 0$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:Q-energy-identity}
	For any solution of the $J$-flow on $[0,T)$ and any $0 \le t_1 \le t_2 < T$, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal{J} (g(t_2)) - \mathcal{J} (g(t_1)) = 4 \int_{t_1}^{t_2} \int_M |J_{g(t)}|_{g(t)}^2 dv_{g(t)} dt - \int_{t_1}^{t_2} \int_M Q_{g(t)}^2 dv_{g(t)} dt .
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rmk:traceless-gradient}
	Decomposing a variation $h$ into its trace part $\frac{1}{n}(\operatorname{tr}_g h)g$ and its traceless part $\mathring h$, the traceless contribution to $D \mathcal{J}_g \cdot h$ is
	\begin{equation*}
		-2 \int_M \langle \mathring J_g,\mathring h \rangle_g dv_g, \qquad \mathring J_g = J_g - \frac{1}{n} Q_g g .
	\end{equation*}
	In dimension four, $\mathring J_g = -\frac{1}{2} B_g$, so the trace-free part of $\partial_t g$ is $(\partial_t g)^\circ = B_g$. Thus the Bach tensor governs the transverse-to-conformal variation of $\mathcal{J}$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:gap}
	Every metric on a closed four-manifold satisfies
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:topological-energy-identity}
		\int_M |\mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g = 32 \pi^2 \chi(M) + 4 \int_M |E|^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation}
	If $M$ is simply connected, then
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M |\mathrm{Rm}|^2 dv_g \ge 64 \pi^2.
	\end{equation*}
	Equality holds precisely when $E = 0$ and $b_2(M) = 0$. In particular this lower bound applies to both $J$-Einstein and $J$-flat metrics.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
In dimension four,
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal J(g) = -\frac{1}{2} \int_M |E|^2 dv_g + \frac{1}{24} \int_M R^2 dv_g.
	\end{equation*}
	Multiplying \Cref{eq:CGB-4} by four and subtracting the result from the decomposition of $|\mathrm{Rm}|^2$ gives \Cref{eq:topological-energy-identity}. A simply connected closed four-manifold is orientable and has $\chi(M) = 2+b_2(M)$. Both remaining terms are nonnegative, proving the bound and its equality statement.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:conformal-invariance}
	The total $Q$-curvature $\mathcal{J}(g)$ is conformally invariant in dimension four, if $\tilde g = e^{2u} g$, then $\mathcal{J}(\tilde g) = \mathcal{J}(g)$.
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Under a conformal change $\tilde g = e^{2u}g$ the $Q$-curvature transforms according to
	\begin{equation*}
		Q_{\tilde g} = \left(P_g u + Q_g \right) e^{-4u}, \qquad dv_{\tilde g} = e^{4u} dv_g,
	\end{equation*}
	where $P_g$ is the Paneitz operator, which is formally self-adjoint and satisfies $P_g 1 = 0$. Therefore
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal{J} (\tilde g) = \int_M (P_gu + Q_g) dv_g = \int_M u P_g1 dv_g + \mathcal{J} (g) = \mathcal{J} (g).
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:Weyl-monotone}
	Let $g(t)$ be a solution of the $J$-flow on a closed four-manifold. Then the Weyl energy
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal{W} (g) := \int_{M^4} |W_g|_g^2 dv_g
	\end{equation*}
	is non-increasing in time.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:Yamabe-propagation}
	Let $(M^4, g_0)$ be a closed connected Riemannian four-manifold, and let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^4 \times [0,T)$ with $g(0) = g_0$. If $Y(M, [g_0])> 0$ and $\mathcal J(g_0) > 0$, then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Yamabe-Q-lower-bound}
		Y(M,[g(t)]) \ge \sqrt{24 \mathcal J(g(t))} \ge \sqrt{24 \mathcal J(g_0)} > 0
	\end{equation}
	for $0 \le t < T$. No curvature bound or uniform Sobolev inequality along the solution is required for this conclusion.
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
By \Cref{thm:Q-evolution}, $\mathcal J(g(t)) \ge \mathcal J(g_0) > 0$ throughout the smooth existence interval. We first show that the first eigenvalue of the conformal Laplacian $-6 \Delta_{g(t)} + R_{g(t)}$ remains positive. At $t = 0$, the Yamabe inequality and H\"older's inequality give
	\begin{equation*}
		\lambda_1( -6 \Delta_{g_0} + R_{g_0}) \ge \frac{ Y(M,[g_0]) }{ \mathrm{Vol}_{g_0}(M)^{\frac{1}{2}}} > 0.
	\end{equation*}
	The first eigenvalue is continuous on every compact time interval contained in $[0,T)$. If positivity failed, there would be a first time $t_*<T$ at which this eigenvalue is zero. Its first eigenfunction $v$ is smooth and strictly positive, since $M$ is connected. Therefore, we have 
	\begin{equation*}
		(-6 \Delta_{g(t_*)} + R_{g(t_*)}) v = 0,
	\end{equation*}
	and using $v$ as a conformal factor, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		R_{v^2 g(t_*)} = v^{-3}(-6 \Delta_{g(t_*)} + R_{g(t_*)}) v = 0.
	\end{equation*}
	Conformal invariance from \Cref{prop:conformal-invariance} and the four-dimensional formula for $Q$ then imply
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal J(g(t_*)) = \mathcal J(v^2 g (t_*)) = -\frac{1}{2} \int_M |\mathrm{Ric}_{v^2 g (t_*)}|^2 dv_{v^2 g(t_*)} \le 0,
	\end{equation*}
	contradicting $\mathcal J(g(t_*)) > 0$. Hence the first eigenvalue remains positive. At each time its positive first eigenfunction produces a conformal representative with everywhere positive scalar curvature. On that fixed closed representative, the positive minimum of the scalar curvature and the ordinary Sobolev inequality make the Yamabe quotient positive. By conformal invariance of the Yamabe constant, $Y(M,[g(t)]) > 0$. This argument is applied at each time separately and assumes no uniform control of the auxiliary representatives.

	Fix $t < T$. The Yamabe theorem~\cite{Schoen1984} supplies a unit-volume metric $\widehat g$ in $[g(t)]$ with constant scalar curvature $R_{\widehat g} = Y(M,[g(t)])$. No continuous choice of these metrics is needed. Integrating the Laplacian term in $Q$ gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\mathcal J(g(t)) 
			= \mathcal J(\widehat g) 
			= \frac{1}{24} \int_M R_{\widehat g}^2 dv_{\widehat g} - \frac{1}{2} \int_M \left|\mathrm{Ric}_{\widehat g} - \frac{1}{4} R_{\widehat g}\widehat g \right|^2 dv_{\widehat g}
			\le \frac{1}{24} Y(M,[g(t)])^2.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Taking square roots and using the monotonicity of $\mathcal J$ proves \Cref{eq:Yamabe-Q-lower-bound}.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rmk:Yamabe-Sobolev-scope}
	The lower bound in \Cref{eq:Yamabe-Q-lower-bound} increases with $\mathcal J$, but it does not establish monotonicity of the Yamabe constant itself. It gives, for every smooth $u$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\|u\|_{4,g(t)}^2 \le \frac{1}{\sqrt{24 \mathcal J(g_0)}} \left( 6 \|\nabla u \|_{2,g(t)}^2 + \int_M (R_{g(t)})_+ u^2 dv_{g(t)}\right),
	\end{equation*}
	where $(R_g)_+=\max\{R_g,0\}$. On the original metrics, this estimate alone does not bound the last integral by a time-independent multiple of $\|u\|_2^2$, a uniform upper bound for $(R_{g(t)})_+$ would suffice. The rescaled metrics used in the proof of \Cref{theorem:D} admit such a bound, as explained in \Cref{rmk:Yamabe-curvature-scale}. Moreover, positivity of $\mathcal J(g_0)$ forces the maximal original flow to have finite lifetime, since
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathrm{Vol}_{g(t)}(M) \le \mathrm{Vol}_{g_0}(M)-t\mathcal J(g_0), \qquad T_{\max} \le \frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{g_0}(M)}{\mathcal J(g_0)}.
	\end{equation*}
	Thus \Cref{prop:Yamabe-propagation} controls the conformal classes up to the smooth existence endpoint, it does not assert long-time existence.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rmk:Yamabe-curvature-scale}
	Suppose that a four-dimensional solution satisfies $Y(M, [g(t)]) \ge Y_*>0$ throughout its maximal existence interval. This lower bound supplies the Sobolev inequality needed in the rescaling argument of \Cref{theorem:D}. Indeed, the rescaled solutions $\hat g_i$ in that proof satisfy $|\mathrm{Rm}_{\hat g_i(s)}|\le 1$ for $0 \le s \le1$. Scale invariance of the Yamabe constant and \Cref{eq:Rm-norm} give
	\begin{equation*}
		Y(M,[\hat g_i(1)])=Y(M,[g(t_i)]) \ge Y_*, \qquad |R_{\hat g_i(1)}| \le \sqrt{6}.
	\end{equation*}
	Consequently, the defining Yamabe inequality implies
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\|u\|_{4, \hat g_i(1)}^2 \le \frac{1}{Y_*} \left(6 \|\nabla u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2+ \sqrt{6} \|u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2 \right) \le \frac{6}{Y_*} \left( \|\nabla u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2 + \|u\|_{2, \hat g_i(1)}^2 \right).
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Thus \Cref{lem:integral-pointwise} applies with $C_S = \frac{6}{Y_*}$, and the proof of \Cref{theorem:D} proceeds with the corresponding small-energy threshold, provided the curvature-energy smallness assumption is retained throughout the maximal interval. The curvature bound used here follows from the choice of the first maximum times and requires no prior global curvature estimate. In \Cref{theorem:E}, the assumed bound $|\mathrm{Rm}| \le K$ gives directly
	\begin{equation*}
		\|u\|_{4,g(t)}^2 \le \frac{1}{Y_*} \left(6 \|\nabla u \|_{2,g(t)}^2 + \sqrt{6} K \|u\|_{2,g(t)}^2 \right),
	\end{equation*}
	so a uniform positive Yamabe lower bound also supplies its Sobolev hypothesis, for example with $r_0 = 1$ and $C_S = \frac{6 + \sqrt{6} K}{Y_*}$.

	There is, however, a restriction on the possible energy thresholds. Taking $u=1$ in the Yamabe quotient and using \Cref{eq:Rm-norm} yields
	\begin{equation*}
		Y_* \le Y(M,[g(t)]) \le \frac{\int_M R_{g(t)} dv_{g(t)}}{\mathrm{Vol}_{g(t)}(M)^{\frac{1}{2}}} \le \| R_{g(t)} \|_{2,g(t)} \le \sqrt{6} \|\mathrm{Rm}_{g(t)}\|_{2,g(t)}, 
	\end{equation*}
	which implies
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M |\mathrm{Rm}_{g(t)}|^2dv_{g(t)} \ge \frac{Y_*^2}{6}.
	\end{equation*}
	A strict small-energy assumption with threshold at most $\frac{Y_*^2}{6}$ would therefore be empty. The substitution above explains the analytic role of the Yamabe lower bound, but does not by itself give a stronger small-energy theorem. The Sobolev formulations in \Cref{theorem:D,theorem:E} also allow Yamabe constant zero, including flat metrics on tori, and are retained for this reason.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- proposition-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:total-Q-high-dimensional-decrease}
	Let $(M^n,\bar g)$ be a closed connected flat manifold with $n > 4$, and let $\varphi$ be a nonzero smooth function satisfying $\int_M \varphi dv_{\bar g} = 0$. Set
	\begin{equation*}
		g_0^\varepsilon = \left( \frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{\bar g}(M)}{ \mathrm{Vol}_{e^{2 \varepsilon \varphi}\bar g}(M)} \right)^{\frac{2}{n}} e^{2 \varepsilon \varphi}\bar g,
	\end{equation*}
	and let $g^\varepsilon(t)$ be the smooth $J$-flow with initial metric $g_0^\varepsilon$, provided by \Cref{theorem:A}. Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:total-Q-high-dimensional-decrease}
		\left. \frac{d}{dt} \mathcal J(g^\varepsilon(t)) \right|_{t=0} = -\frac{3(n-4)}{4(n-1)} \varepsilon^2 \int_M ( \Delta_{\bar g}^2 \varphi )^2 dv_{\bar g} + O(\varepsilon^3).
	\end{equation}
	In particular, the derivative is strictly negative for every sufficiently small nonzero $\varepsilon$.
<!-- end proposition-15 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
All derivatives and norms in this proof are taken with respect to $\bar g$. $\int_M \varphi dv_{\bar g} = 0$ implies that the volume has zero first derivative at $\varepsilon = 0$. Consequently $\left.\partial_\varepsilon g_0^\varepsilon \right|_{\varepsilon = 0} = 2 \varphi \bar g$. The conformal variations of Ricci and scalar curvature at the flat background are
	\begin{equation*}
		D\mathrm{Ric}_{\bar g}[2 \varphi \bar g] = -(n-2) \nabla^2 \varphi - (\Delta \varphi) \bar g, \qquad DR_{\bar g}[2 \varphi\bar g] = -2(n-1) \Delta \varphi.
	\end{equation*}
	The quadratic curvature terms in \Cref{eq:intro-Q,eq:J-full} have zero linearization at $\bar g$, as do variations of the differential operators acting on its vanishing curvature. Substitution therefore gives
	\begin{equation*}
		D Q_{\bar g}[2 \varphi \bar g] = \Delta^2\varphi, \qquad
		D J_{\bar g}[2 \varphi \bar g] = \frac{n-4}{4(n-1)} \nabla^2 \Delta \varphi + \frac{3}{4(n-1)}(\Delta^2 \varphi)\bar g.
	\end{equation*}
	Flatness and integration by parts give
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M |\nabla^2 \Delta \varphi|^2 dv_{\bar g} = \int_M (\Delta^2 \varphi)^2 dv_{\bar g}.
	\end{equation*}
	Expanding the square thus yields
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\int_M \left| DJ_{\bar g}[2 \varphi \bar g] \right|^2 dv_{\bar g} 
			= \frac{(n-4)^2+6(n-4)+9n}{16(n-1)^2} \int_M (\Delta^2 \varphi)^2 dv_{\bar g} 
			= \frac{n+8}{16(n-1)} \int_M (\Delta^2 \varphi)^2 dv_{\bar g}.
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Since $J_{\bar g} = Q_{\bar g} = 0$, Taylor expansion of \Cref{eq:Q-evolution} at $g_0^\varepsilon$ gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{split}
			\left.\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal J(g^\varepsilon(t))\right|_{t=0}
			& = \varepsilon^2 \left(4 \int_M \left| D J_{\bar g}[2 \varphi \bar g] \right|^2 dv_{\bar g} - \int_M \left| D Q_{\bar g}[2 \varphi \bar g] \right|^2 dv_{\bar g} \right) + O(\varepsilon^3) \\
			& = \left(\frac{n+8}{4(n-1)} - 1 \right) \varepsilon^2 \int_M( \Delta^2 \varphi)^2 dv_{\bar g} + O(\varepsilon^3).
		\end{split}
	\end{equation*}
	Variations of the contractions and volume density contribute only to the remainder, since the curvature tensors being squared vanish at $\varepsilon = 0$. This proves \Cref{eq:total-Q-high-dimensional-decrease}. Finally, $\Delta^2 \varphi = 0$ would imply $\int_M (\Delta \varphi)^2 dv_{\bar g} = 0$, so $\varphi$ would be constant and hence zero. The leading integral is therefore strictly positive.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rmk:normalized-Yamabe-and-Q}
	Both conclusions hold for the volume-normalized equation as well. In dimension $n\ge4$, this equation is
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\partial g}{\partial t} = -2 J_g + \frac{2}{n} \bar Q_g g, \qquad \bar Q_g = \frac{\mathcal J(g)}{\mathrm{Vol}_g(M)},
	\end{equation*}
	which agrees with \Cref{eq:normalized-J-flow} when $n = 4$. By \Cref{eq:first-variation-2}, its normalization term adds
	\begin{equation*}
		D\mathcal J_g \left[ \frac{2}{n} \bar Q_g g \right] = \frac{n-4}{n} \mathrm{Vol}_g(M) \bar Q_g^2
	\end{equation*}
	to \Cref{eq:Q-evolution}. This contribution is zero in dimension four, so the proof and conclusion of \Cref{prop:Yamabe-propagation} remain valid. For the initial metrics in \Cref{prop:total-Q-high-dimensional-decrease}, $\mathcal J(g_0^\varepsilon) = O(\varepsilon^2)$ because $Q_{\bar g} = 0$ and $\int_M \Delta^2 \varphi dv_{\bar g} = 0$. The added term is therefore $O(\varepsilon^4)$, leaving the strictly negative second-order term in \Cref{eq:total-Q-high-dimensional-decrease} unchanged.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proposition-16: body_md -->
\label{prop:J-flat-char}
	A Riemannian metric $(M^4, g)$ is $J$-flat if and only if it is Bach-flat and has zero $Q$-curvature.
<!-- end proposition-16 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Taking the trace and using that the Bach tensor is traceless in four dimensions gives $\operatorname{tr}_g J_g = Q_g$. Then the result is trivial.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
By \Cref{prop:J-Schur}, the definition $J = \Lambda(x) g$ is equivalent to Bach-flatness in dimension four. Requiring $\Lambda$ to be constant imposes the additional condition that $Q$ is constant. For example, on a flat four-torus any metric $g = e^{2u} g_{\mathrm{flat}}$ is Bach-flat, whereas $Q_g = e^{-4u} \Delta_{g_{\mathrm{flat}}}^2u$ is generally nonconstant. Thus the Schur conclusion cannot be applied in dimension four. The characterization of $J$-flatness in \Cref{prop:J-flat-char} does require $Q = 0$.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
The round sphere is not $J$-flat because its $Q$-curvature is a positive constant. Consequently there is no simply connected closed $J$-flat four-manifold with small total curvature: Chern--Gauss--Bonnet gives $\int_M |W|^2 dv = 32 \pi^2 \chi(M) \ge 64 \pi^2$ for any simply connected $J$-flat metric. The stronger bound in \Cref{prop:gap} is a topological statement for all metrics.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:compact-J-limit}
	For an immortal $J$-flow on a closed four-manifold, $\mathcal J(g(t)) \le 0$ for every $t$. If its volume is uniformly bounded above, then $\mathcal J(g(t)) \to 0$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
If $\mathcal J(g(t_1)) > 0$ at any time, monotonicity gives $\mathrm{Vol}'(t) \le -\mathcal J(g(t_1))$ for $t \ge t_1$, which makes the volume negative in finite time. Hence $\mathcal J \le 0$. Its increasing limit $L$ exists. If the volume is bounded above and $L < 0$, then $\mathrm{Vol}'(t) = -\mathcal J(g(t)) \ge -L > 0$, contradicting that bound. Therefore $L = 0$ in this case.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:Bach-decay}
	Let $g(t)$ be an immortal $J$-flow on a closed four-manifold. Suppose that, for some $t_0 > 0$, $\{g(t):t \ge t_0\}$ has compact closure in the $C^\infty$ topology of positive definite metrics on the fixed manifold $M$. Then $\|B_{g(t)}\|_{C^k( \bar{g} )} \to 0$ for every $k \ge 0$ and any fixed smooth background metric $\bar{g}$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Compact closure gives a uniform upper bound for the volume, uniform bounds on all fixed-coordinate derivatives of $g$ and $g^{-1}$, and uniform equivalence to $\bar{g}$. The flow equation also bounds all fixed-coordinate derivatives of $\partial_tg$. By \Cref{lem:compact-J-limit} and \Cref{eq:Q-mono-4D},
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{t_0}^\infty \|B_{g(t)}\|_2^2 dt = - \mathcal J(g(t_0))<\infty.
	\end{equation*}
	The Bach tensor is a differential expression of order four in $g$. The uniform bounds on the metric, its derivatives through order eight, and $\partial_tg$ and its derivatives through order four give a uniform bound for $\left| \frac{d}{dt} \|B_{g(t)}\|_2^2 \right|$. Therefore $\|B_{g(t)}\|_2^2 \to 0$, since a nonnegative integrable function with bounded derivative tends to zero, otherwise uniform continuity supplies disjoint intervals of definite length on which its integral is bounded below.

	To obtain every $C^k$ conclusion, suppose there were a sequence $t_i \to \infty$ along which the asserted norm stayed positive. Compactness gives a smoothly convergent subsequence of $g(t_i)$, continuity of $B$ and the vanishing $L^2$ norm make its limiting Bach tensor zero, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proposition-17: body_md -->
\label{prop:compact-sequential-limits}
	Let $(M^4, g_0)$ be a closed Riemannian four-manifold, and let $g(t)$ be a smooth solution of the $J$-flow on $M^4 \times [0,\infty)$ with $g(0) = g_0$. Suppose that, for some $t_0 > 0$, $\{g(t): t \ge t_0\}$ has compact closure in the $C^\infty$ topology of positive definite metrics on the fixed manifold $M$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal J(g(t)) \nearrow 0, \qquad \|B_{g(t)}\|_{C^k(\bar{g})} \longrightarrow 0
	\end{equation*}
	for every $k \ge 0$ and any fixed smooth background metric $\bar{g}$. Every sequence $t_i \to \infty$ has a subsequence along which $g(t_i)$ converges smoothly to a positive definite Bach-flat metric with total $Q$-curvature zero. If $\| Q_{g(t)} \|_{L^2(g(t))}\to 0$, every such limit is $J$-flat.
<!-- end proposition-17 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Compact closure bounds the volume above, so \Cref{lem:compact-J-limit,lem:Bach-decay} give $\mathcal J  \nearrow  0$ and $B \to 0$. Compact closure provides a smoothly convergent subsequence from every sequence of times tending to infinity. Each limit is Bach-flat with total $Q$-curvature zero. If $\|Q\|_2 \to 0$, continuity under smooth metric convergence gives $Q = 0$ at each limit, and \Cref{prop:J-flat-char} makes it $J$-flat.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:Bach-time-integrability}
	Let $g(t)$ be an immortal $J$-flow on a closed four-manifold satisfying \Cref{eq:LS-hypothesis-F} for all sufficiently large $t$. Then $\mathcal J(g(t)) \nearrow 0$, and for all sufficiently large $a$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Bach-tail-estimate}
		\int_a^\infty \|B_{g(t)}\|_2 dt \le \frac{C_{\mathrm L}}{\theta} \left(-\mathcal J(g(a)) \right)^\theta.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
By \Cref{lem:compact-J-limit} and \Cref{eq:Q-mono-4D}, $\mathcal J(g(t)) \le 0$ and
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{d}{dt} \mathcal J(g(t)) = \| B_{g(t)} \|_2^2 \ge 0.
	\end{equation*}
	Its increasing limit is zero: a negative limit and \Cref{eq:LS-hypothesis-F} would give a positive lower bound for $\| B_{g(t)} \|_2$, forcing $\mathcal J(g(t))$ to become positive. On intervals where $\mathcal J(g(t)) < 0$ and \Cref{eq:LS-hypothesis-F} holds, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		-\frac{d}{dt} \left(-\mathcal J(g(t)) \right)^\theta = \theta \left(-\mathcal J(g(t))\right)^{\theta-1} \|B_{g(t)}\|_2^2 \ge \frac{\theta}{C_{\mathrm L}} \|B_{g(t)}\|_2.
	\end{equation*}
	Integrating from $a$ to infinity and using $\mathcal J(g(t)) \to 0$ proves the estimate when $\mathcal J(g(t))$ stays negative. If it reaches zero at a finite time, it remains zero by its nonpositivity and monotonicity, and then $\| B_{g(t)} \|_2 = 0$ thereafter. Integrating up to that time proves the estimate in this case as well.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
The proof of \Cref{theorem:D} gives a uniform curvature bound on $[0, \infty)$, and the uniform Sobolev inequality is part of its hypotheses. It remains to verify that the $L^2$-norm of $J$ is integrable in time, as required by \Cref{theorem:E}. Since $B$ is trace free and $|g|_g^2 = 4$, \Cref{eq:J-dim4} gives
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:velocity-orthogonal-F}
		4\|J\|_{L^2(g(t))}^2 = \|\partial_t g\|_{L^2(g(t))}^2 = \|B\|_{L^2(g(t))}^2 + \|Q\|_{L^2(g(t))}^2.
	\end{equation}
	For all sufficiently large $a$, \Cref{lem:Bach-time-integrability,eq:Q-time-integrability-F} therefore imply
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-tail-estimate-F}
		\int_a^\infty\|J\|_2 dt \le \frac{C_{\mathrm L}}{2\theta} \left(-\mathcal J(g(a)) \right)^\theta + \frac{1}{2} \int_a^\infty \|Q\|_2 dt < \infty.
	\end{equation}
	Since the solution is smooth on the compact cylinder $M \times [0, a]$, the integral of $\|J\|_2$ over $[0, a]$ is finite. Combining this with \Cref{eq:J-tail-estimate-F} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^\infty \|J_{g(t)}\|_{L^2(g(t))} dt < \infty.
	\end{equation*}
	In particular, \Cref{eq:J-time-integrability} holds, and \Cref{theorem:E} gives smooth convergence in fixed coordinates to a positive definite $J$-flat metric.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{cor:Bach-Q-integrability}
	In \Cref{theorem:F}, one may replace the gradient inequality by
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^\infty \|B_{g(t)}\|_2 dt < \infty.
	\end{equation*}
	The hypotheses of \Cref{theorem:D} and the time-integrability condition on the $L^2$-norm of $Q$ in \Cref{eq:Q-time-integrability-F} are retained.
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
By \Cref{eq:velocity-orthogonal-F}, these assumptions give
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^\infty \|J\|_2 dt \le \frac{1}{2} \int_0^\infty \left( \|B\|_2 + \|Q\|_2 \right) dt < \infty.
	\end{equation*}
	Thus \Cref{eq:J-time-integrability} holds. The curvature bound from the proof of \Cref{theorem:D} and its Sobolev hypothesis allow us to apply \Cref{theorem:E}.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- proposition-18: body_md -->
\label{prop:compact-J-soliton}
    Let $(M^4,g)$ be a closed connected Riemannian four-manifold satisfying \Cref{eq:J-soliton}, then every such soliton is trivial with constant Q curvature and Bach-flat. Furthermore, if $X = \nabla f$, then $f$ is constant. Conversely, every Bach-flat metric with constant $Q$-curvature satisfies \Cref{eq:J-soliton} with $\lambda = \frac{1}{4} Q_g$ and any Killing field $X$.
<!-- end proposition-18 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
By \Cref{prop:trace-J,prop:J-Schur}, the tensor $J_g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g$ is trace free and satisfies
    \begin{equation*}
        \operatorname{div}_g \left( J_g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g \right) = \frac{1}{4} d Q_g - \frac{1}{4}d Q_g = 0.
    \end{equation*}
    Pairing \Cref{eq:J-soliton} with this tensor and integrating by parts gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \begin{split}
            \int_M \left\langle J_g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g, J_g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g \right\rangle dv_g
            & = \int_M \left\langle J_g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g, \mathcal \lambda g - \frac{1}{2} L_X g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g \right\rangle dv_g\\
			& = \int_M \left\langle J_g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g, \mathcal  - \frac{1}{2} L_X g  \right\rangle dv_g\\
            & = \int_M \operatorname{div}_g \left( J_g - \frac{1}{4} Q_g g \right)(X) d v_g = 0.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus $J_g = \frac{1}{4} Q_g g$, and $B_g = 0$. Substitution into the soliton equation yields
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal L_X g = 2 \left( \lambda - \frac{1}{4} Q_g \right) g,
    \end{equation*}
    so $X$ is a conformal vector field. The Kazdan--Warner identity for $Q$-curvature on a closed manifold~\cite[Proposition~4.2]{GoverOrsted2013} gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_M X(Q_g) dv_g = 0.
    \end{equation*}
    On the other hand, taking trace of \Cref{eq:J-soliton} and integrating gives
    \begin{equation*}
        Q_g + \operatorname{div}_g X = 4 \lambda.
    \end{equation*}
    Consequently,
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_M (Q_g - 4 \lambda)^2 dv_g = \int_M Q_g (Q_g - 4 \lambda) dv_g = - \int_M Q_g \operatorname{div}_g X dv_g = \int_M X(Q_g) dv_g = 0.
	\end{equation*}
    Hence $Q_g = 4 \lambda$ and $\mathcal L_X g = 0$. If $X = \nabla f$, then $\nabla^2 f=0$, so $\Delta_g f = 0$ and $f$ is constant by closeness and connectedness. The converse follows from \Cref{eq:J-dim4}.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
